PREFACE 

This book was prepared for the purpose of furnishing a source book for the lawyers, tax accountants, and others interested in the operation of the Oklahoma Community Property Law. It is hoped they may find in its pages solutions to the many problems which will inevitably arise in connection with the operation of this law. This book cannot be authoritative in any sense. The most it can do is supply the reader with authorities from the eight other states which have this system of property and a careful discussion of our Community Property Law as it may be applied under the guidance of the decisions of these other states. 

Inasmuch as the Supreme Court of the State of Oklahoma has not construed any of the provisions of the Oklahoma Community Property Law, the real object of this book is to lay before the reader a large number of selected authorities which the authors believe will be persuasive upon our courts in construing provisions of our law which resemble the provisions of the laws of the other states operating under a community property system. We have cited the decisions of those courts extensively and have quoted their exact language rather than replace it with what we might think was the correct interpretation of such decisions. 

If the user of this book will take the decisions we have cited and trace them by means of a Shephard's Citator, he will have access to all decisions construing the Community Property Law in any state in the United States. 

No attempt has been made to cite the controlling decisions in the other states. We have selected only such cases as we feel will be applicable in Oklahoma without regard to whether or not such decisions have been later modified or overruled by the courts of the state from which these decisions have been selected.

It is impossible to mention by name all persons who have assisted the authors in the preparation of this book. However, we wish to thank Mr. J. D. Carmichael, Chairman of the Oklahoma Tax Commission, who first suggested this book and whose cooperation and kindness has enabled the authors to complete this work within a limited time; the law firm of 

Twyford, Smith & Crowe of Oklahoma City for kind permission to quote extensively from articles prepared by them on the transmutation of property and inheritance taxes; Mr. 

Harry Campbell of Tulsa, Oklahoma, for his generous help and permission to use the Income Tax Tables prepared by him and printed in full in the appendix of this book; Mr. A. D. 

Howell of the Oklahoma Tax Commission for his numerous suggestions and illuminating discussions of the problems surrounding the Oklahoma Community Property Law; Mr. Ralph 

Hudson, State Librarian, for placing the facilities of his staff and the State Library at the disposal of the authors; and Miss 

Alice Bridges for constant and continuous assistance in the mechanical preparation of this book. 

-The Authors

Chapter One GENERAL INTRODUCTION Section 1-History. 

According to the best authorities, the community property system, a type of property unknown to the English Common Law or the Roman Law, was created by the ancient German tribes. It was carried by the migrating Visigoths into France and Spain during their irruptions against the Romans prior to the fall of their empire. It took root in Spain under the long domination of that country by the Goths and affected the French law. It was brought to America by the early Spanish and 

French settlers in Mexico and Louisiana. From these two points it has spread through Texas, New Mexico, Arizona, California, and up the west coast to Washington, Idaho and Nevada. 

All of these states have constitutional and statutory enactments governing this type of property; some based upon underlying customs, others created by legislative enactment without any background such as prevailed in Texas, California and Louisiana. In each of the states having either Spanish or 

French customs, a long struggle has been waged between the English common law theories of property and those of the community system. The result has been to form something different in each state from the strict community type known in 

Spain and France.

As community property in Oklahoma is purely statutory and was created completely by one legislative act in 1939 (unless the Oklahoma homestead partakes somewhat of community property), it is unnecessary to comment further on the history of community property. 1

Section 2-Definitions. 

No satisfactory definition of community property has been given either in the text books or decisions of the courts which can be relied upon as a safe guide. It is believed that a nega- (tive definition is all that can be given. Under this idea, community property is defined as all property owned by a man and his wife which is not their separate property. This includes both real and personal property of whatever kind. It is a type of property arising out of the marriage relation, and does not exist in the absence of marriage. However, in Oklahoma, community property does not arise by reason of marriage; it is created by a written election filed by husband and wife with the county clerk and Secretary of State. In this the Oklahoma community property system differs from that of the other states. 

Courts and text writers have attempted to differentiate the various types of community property as they exist in the several states, classifying the underlying theories into four categories: 

The single ownership theory of California;

The entity theory of Washington; 

The double ownership theory of Idaho; and, The trust theory which prevails in Texas. 

Under the single ownership theory of California, community property did not set up until after the death of the husband. 

A long line of California decisions hold that the wife has only an expectancy which does not ripen until after the death of her husband. By section 161a, Civil Code of California, enacted in 

1927, this theory may be considerably altered. See Sampson v. 

Welch, 23 Fed. Supp. 271. However, it cannot prevail in Oklahoma under our present statute. Our act creating community property gives the wife a vested undivided one-half interest in the community property immediately upon coming under the provisions of the act.

The Washington theory holds that the spouses constitute an entity separate and distinct from each other. The husband is the agent of the entity. In many respects this theory creates a type of corporation. As this concept will not likely be adopted in Oklahoma, it will not be discussed further. 

The double ownership theory developed by the courts of

the State of Idaho, the theory which Oklahoma will perhaps follow, is the most sensible and workable of any of the theories. This concept includes tenancy in common and partnership holding of property. These are familiar to Oklahoma law and under the wording of the act creating community property in Oklahoma that community property must be administered by the surviving spouse in the manner and form in which a surviving partner administers partnership affairs, and that after such administration, such property shall be held by surviving spouse as a tenant in common it is believed the Idaho theory will prevail.

While text writers speak of the trust theory as dominant in Texas, that state has added other elements to such property in the nature of partnership so both theories may be said to exist side by side. The decisions use first one and the other concept in deciding matters of community property. 

Section 3-Purpose of Law. 

The object of the adoption of the community property system, in such states as have enacted it into law without finding it in their customs, has been to soften the rigors and hardships of the common law property as to the wife. It has sought to place her more nearly on an equality with the husband on the basis that she has contributed by her labor and industry to the creation of all property acquired after marriage. It attempts to recognize her as an equal factor in the accumulation of property and protect her against the absolute control of such property by the husband. )

However, an examination of the statutes and decisions of the various states will show this object has not been accomplished. In all of the states the husband is given superior control over the community property and may sell and dispose of same, limited only by the usual rule that such disposition must not be in fraud of the wife's rights.

The Oklahoma law comes nearest to giving the wife an equal control over the community, yet it gives the husband a measure of control denied to the wife. She controls that part of the community property which stands in her name; he controls not only the property standing in his name, but other property held by joint title.

Section 4-The Community Property Act. 

In order to furnish the reader with a comprehensive view of the Oklahoma Community Law, we print House Bill 565 of the Seventeenth Legislature, article 2, chapter 62, Session 

Laws 1939, in full: 

"Section 1. Husband and Wife-Election. This Act shall be available to and apply only to husbands and wives and to their property for a period of time from the first day of the month in any year subsequent to the filing their written election to come under the terms of this Act until either an absolute decree of divorce is rendered dissolving their marriage, or until the death of one of them. 

"Section 2. Written Election-Filing of Instrument. The written election to come under the terms of this Act, referred to in Section 1 of this Act, shall be a written instrument signed and acknowledged in duplicate by both husband and wife, stating in substance that they desire to avail themselves of the Act and have same apply to them and to their property on the first day of the next month in any year subsequent to the filing thereof in both the office of the county clerk and the Secretary of State as hereinafter provided. Acknowledgments shall be in the form, and may be taken before any officer now prescribed by law for acknowledgments to conveyances of real estate. One of the said written instruments shall be filed in the office of the county clerk of the county of the residence of the signers thereof, and one in the office of the Secretary of State. The county clerks and the Secretary of State shall cause all such instruments to be recorded in records kept for that purpose, and to be properly indexed. 

"Section 3. Separate Property of Husband. All property, both real and personal, of the husband owned or claimed by him before the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act, as provided in Section 1 of this Act, and that acquired afterwards by gift, including gifts of the wife's interest in community property, by division of community property, by devise, or by descent, as also the increase of all lands thus owned or acquired, shall be his separate property. The separate property of the husband shall not be subject to the debts contracted by the wife or liable for her torts, either before or after the effective date of said election, except as may be permitted by law as to his property prior to the enactment of this Act. The

husband shall have the sole management, control and disposition of his separate property, both real and personal, to the extent permitted by law as to his property prior to the enactment of this Act. 

"Section 4. Separate Property of Wife. All property, both real and personal, of the wife owned or claimed by her before the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act as provided in Section 1 of this Act, and that acquired afterwards by gift, including gifts of the husband's interest in community property, by division of community property, by devise, or by descent, as also the increase of all lands thus owned or acquired, shall be her separate property. The separate property of the wife shall not be subject to the debts contracted by the husband or liable for his torts, either before or after the effective date of said election, except as may be permitted by law as to her property prior to the enactment of this Act. The wife shall have the sole management, control and dispostion of her separate property, both real and personal, to the extent permitted by law as to her property prior to the enactment of this Act. 

"Section 5. Compensation for Personal Injuries. All prop erty or moneys received as compensation for personal injuries sustained either by the husband or the wife shall be the separate property of the person sustaining such injuries. 

"Section 6. Property Acquired After Effective Date of Act. 

All property acquired by the husband or the wife after the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act as provided in Section 1 of this Act, except that which is separate property of either one or the other, shall be deemed the community or common property of the husband and the wife and each, subject to the provisions of this Act, shall be vested with an undivided one-half interest therein. The wife shall have the management and control and may dispose of that portion of the community property consisting of her earnings, all rents, interest, dividends, incomes and other profits for her separate estate and all other community property the title to which stands in her name. The husband shall have the management and control and may dispose of all other community property, provided, however, that the homestead, if community property, shall not be sold, encumbered, or otherwise disposed

of, except in the manner as is provided by law prior to the enactment of this Act, and further provided, that any funds on deposit in any bank or banking institution, whether in the name of the husband or the wife, shall be presumed to be the separate property of the party in whose name they stand, regardless of who made the deposit, and unless said bank or banking institution is notified to the contrary, it shall be governed accordingly in honoring checks and orders against such account. 

"Section 7. Wife-Property of Subject to Her Debts. The separate property of the wife and that portion of community property, record title to which is in her name or which is under the management, control and disposition of the wife shall be subject to debts contracted by the wife arising out of tort, or otherwise, but not to debts or liabilities of the husband. The separate property of the husband and that portion of the community property, record title to which is in his name or which is under the management, control and disposition of the husband shall be subject to debts contracted by the husband or liabilities of the husband arising out of tort or otherwise, but not the debts or liabilities of the wife. The husband and the wife, and each of them, shall be entitled to the exemptions to which they, or either of them, are now entitled under the laws existing prior to the enactment of this Act. "Section 8.

Creditors-Husband and Wife-Rights. No creditor shall have recourse to the community property for the payment of debts or liabilities created by either the husband or the wife, except as provided in Section 7 of this Act; provided, however, that any creditor may satisfy his claim or demand out of the community property which was under the management, control and disposition of the spouse incurring the indebtedness or liability at the time the debt or liability was contracted or created, and which has been subsequently conveyed or transferred to the other spouse and is under the management, control and disposition of said other spouse, without proof that said creditor relied upon said community property in advancing said credit, but without prejudice to the rights of the third party purchasers, incumbrancers, or other creditors or grantees; and provided further, that the husband or wife on paying community debts shall, as between themselves, charge the same against community property. 

"Section 9. Deeds-Husband to Wife and Wife to Hus-

band. The husband may give, grant, bargain, sell or convey directly to his wife, and a wife may give, grant, bargain, sell or convey directly to her husband, his or her community right, title, interest or estate in all or any property of their community real or personal property. Every deed and conveyance made from the husband to the wife or from the wife to the husband shall operate to divest the property therein described of every claim or demand as community property, and shall vest the same in the grantee as the separate property of the grantee; provided, however, that the deeds, conveyances or transfers hereby authorized shall not affect any existing equity in favor of creditors of the grantor at the time of such transfer, gift or encumbrance. 

"Section 10. Divorce-Division of Property. In the event of the dissolution of marriage by decree of any court of competent jurisdiction, community property shall be divided between the parties by the court granting the decree, in such proportions as such court, from the facts in the case, shall deem just and equitable, and such division shall be subject to revision on appeal in all respects including the exercise of discretion by the court below. 

"Section 11. Insanity or Conviction of Felony-Rights of 

Spouse. Whenever the husband or the wife is non compos mentis, or has been convicted of a felony or sentenced to imprisonment for a period of more than one year, or whenever the husband has abandoned his wife and family and left her and his family, if they have children, without support, or whenever the husband or the wife is an habitual drunkard, or for any other reason is incapacitated to manage, control, or dispose of the community property, the other spouse may present a petition, duly verified, to the district court of the county wherein they reside, or if they are nonresidents wherein any of the community property is located or situated, stating the name of the incapacitated spouse, a description of all community property, both real and personal, and the facts which render the other spouse incapacitated to manage, control or dispose of the community property, and praying that the spouse filing the petition be substituted for the incapacitated spouse as to the management, control or disposition of the community property then under the management, control and disposition of said spouse with the same power of managing, controlling and disposing of the community property as was vested in the incapacitated spouse.

"Section 12. Service of Process. In all such cases service of process shall be had as in other civil actions, provided, however, that where it is alleged that the other spouse is non compos mentis, a guardian ad litem shall be appointed having such powers as in other civil actions. 

"Section 13. Hearing on Petition. Upon the hearing of the petition so filed, the court shall render judgment therein either dismissing said petition or adjudging the spouse filing same to have such power of managing, controlling and disposing of the community property, either real or personal, formerly under the management, control and disposition of the other spouse as to the court may appear to be just, proper, equitable, and to the best interest of said estate. "Section 14. Judgments. All judgments rendered as in the preceding Section provided shall be recorded in the office of the county clerk of the county where any property affected thereby is situated and such judgment when so rendered shall be notice of the facts therein set out. 

"Section 15. Death of Husband or Wife. Upon the death of the husband or the wife, the surviving spouse shall administer all community property in the same manner and with the same duties, privileges and authority as are vested in a surviving partner to administer and settle the affairs of a partnership upon the death of the other partner, as provided by Section 1197, Oklahoma Statutes, 1931; provided that the surviving husband or wife shall not be disqualified from acting as executor or administrator of the estate of the deceased husband or wife; and provided further, that the survivor of the husband or wife shall pay out of the community property, except the homestead and exempt property, all debts of the community, whether created by the husband or the wife; and provided further, that when all debts of the community shall have been fully satisfied the survivor shall transfer and convey to the administrator or executor of the deceased one-half of the community property remaining to be administered and distributed as other property of the estate either subject to the terms of the will the deceased or under the laws of descent and distribution as the case may be, and thereafter all the interest of the surviving partner in said community property shall be that of a tenant in common; and provided further, that any interest in a homestead so conveyed shall not be subject to administration under the

laws of this State, except in the manner provided by law at the time of the enactment of this Act." 

Section 5-Construction of Act. 

Inasmuch as community property has not received any construction by our Supreme Court, it will be necessary throughout this book to rely entirely upon the decisions of other states which have had this type of property. The reader should be warned that such decisions are not authoritative, but are persuasive only, and their use in connection with any of the provisions of the Oklahoma law can only be by analogy. In weighing and testing such decisions, the reader should keep clearly in mind the broad distinction which separates Oklahoma community property from that of other states. 

The cases cited herein have been chosen by the authors as being those which in their opinion the Oklahoma Supreme Court will use in settling disputed matters arising under this law. 

They are not exhaustive, but have been selected to indicate how courts in other community property states have reasoned upon situations likely to present similar problems under our law. 

It is probably not necessary to enter a caveat for the general reader against his taking opinions expressed in this work as being something more than opinions. This is a book which attempts only to lay before the reader a study of the community property system as it may operate in Oklahoma. This study has been made in order to present in short compass how community property laws affect husband and wife in all of their legal relations regarding their property after it has been created. 

The statutes and decisions of the eight other states having this system of property have been carefully studied in comparison with the provisions of the Oklahoma law. The Oklahoma courts in solving questions under this law may strike out along an entirely new line, but it is expected they will rely heavily upon what other courts have said and be guided by their reasonings.

Chapter Two CREATION OF COMMUNITY Section 6-Requirements. 

In order to create community property in Oklahoma it is necessary to comply with the following two sections of Article 

2, Chapter 62, Session Laws 1939: 

"Section 1. This Act shall be available only to and apply only to husbands and wives and to their property for a period of time from the first day of the month in any year subsequent to their filling their written election to come under the terms of this Act until either an absolute decree of divorce is rendered dissolving their marriage, or until the death of one of them. "Section 2. The written election to come under the terms of this Act, referred to in Section 1 of this Act, shall be a written instrument signed and acknowledged in duplicate by both husband and wife, stating in substance that they desire to avail themselves of the Act and have same apply to them and to their property on the first day of the next month in any year subsequent to the filing thereof in both the office of the county clerk and the Secretary of State as hereinafter provided. Acknowedgments shall be in the form, and may be taken before any officer now prescribed by law for acknowledgments to conveyances of real estate. One of the said written instruments shall be filed in the office of the county clerk of the county of the residence of the signers thereof, and one in the office of the Secretary of State. The county clerks and the Secretary of State shall cause all such instruments to be recorded in records kept for that purpose, and to be properly indexed." 

These two sections make a written election to come under the community property law an absolute requirement, and restrict it further by fixing the first day of the subsequent month from the date of filing the election as the date at which it commences. This rests community property upon the affirmative act of a husband and wife duly recorded in two public offices. 

Section 7-Form of Election. 

As the statute is silent on the form of the written election

to be used the Attorney General, in answer to an inquiry of the Secretary of State, has supplied a form which covers the essentials believed to be required under this Act. We print the form in full, as follows: "We, husband and wife, who reside in County, -------9 Oklahoma, and whose post office address is -(Set forth post office address in full). do hereby state that we desire to avail ourselves of House Bill 565 of the Seventeenth Legislature of the State of Oklahoma, same being the Oklahoma Community Property Law, and to have said law apply to us and to our property from and after the first day of the next month subsequent to the filing of the duplicate originals hereof, one in the office of the county clerk of the county of our residence and the other in the office of the Secretary of State. "State of Oklahoma, County of "In Witness Whereof, we have hereunto set our hands on this, the day of 19 written. -" 

Before me, a Notary Public, in and for the said county and state, on this day of 19------, personally appeared and 

My commission expires: -SS. -" and husand and wife, to me known to be the identical persons who executed the within and foregoing instrument, and each for themselves acknowledged to me that they executed the same as their free and voluntary act and deed for the uses and purposes therein set forth. "Witness my hand and seal the day and year last above 19........ 99 

Notary Public Section 8-Substantial Compliance Required. 

The above form is not statutory. Persons desiring to avail themselves of the community property law may prepare and

file their own election. Care should be exercised, however, to have their written election conform to the provisions of the statute. A substantial compliance is all that is required. 

From the language of the Act it is clear that community property does not set up until the first day of the month following the date of the last filing of the two copies of the written election. Both instruments must be filed before community property sets up. The date of the last filing governs the time when the Act applies. If the first filing is with the county clerk, the second filing with the Secretary of State fixes the date, and vice versa.

To determine whether or not a man and wife have created community property it will be necessary to examine the files of both the county clerk and the Secretary of State. If they do not file in both offices they do not come under the statute. 

Section 9-Failure to File Election. 

It is believed that failure to complete both filings prevents community rights and obligations in any form and between any parties from coming into existence. Whatever the intention and whatever steps may have been taken to create community property on the part of a husband and wife, unless the written election is duly signed by both of them and properly acknowledged and filed in both offices community property does not attach and their acts are wholly ineffective. 

If husband and wife agree to come under the community property law and work out the details thereof, some of which may be mutual conveyances and assignments in adjusting their various property rights, sign and acknowledge an election, and for any reason, fail to secure a filing in both offices, no rights or duties in relation thereto in so far as community property is concerned are created. Neither party to the agreement, nor a third party, can maintain an action to force completion of such agreement by filing the election, nor claim that community property has been in fact created. 

The above statement is based upon the belief that our Supreme Court will give a strict construction to this Act and hold that community property can only arise by reason of a written election properly filed. If our court should hold that intent plays any part under such statute, then a different construction would be placed on the acts and intents of the parties.

No particular form of language is required to bring the written election within the provisions of the Act. The law says: "stating in substance that they desire to avail themselves of the Act and have same apply to their property." Inasmuch as no standard form of written election will be used, the courts will propably hold that any written request duly signed, acknowledged, and filed will be sufficient to bring the parties under community property law. 

Section 10-Acknowledgment of Election. 

The written election as provided in section two of the Act may be acknowledged before any officer now prescribed by law for acknowledgment to conveyances of real estate. The Oklahoma statutes covering acknowledgments is as follows: "Every acknowledgment must be under seal of the officer taking the same; and when taken in this state, it may be taken before any Notary Public, County Clerk, Clerk of the District Court, Clerk of the County Court, or County Judge; and when taken out of the State, it may be taken before any Notary Public, Clerk of a Court of Record, or Commissioner of Deeds, duly appointed by the Governor of the state for the county, state or territory where the same is taken; and when taken in any foreign country, it may be taken before any Court of Record or Clerk of such Court, or before any consul of the United States 

***" (Sec. 9704, O. S. 1931, Sec. 35, O. S. A., Title 16.) 

The form of acknowledgment must in all instances show person appearing and acknowledging instrument was known to officer to be identical person who executed instrument. Maitland v. Republic Refining Co., 109 Ok. 55, 234 P. 754. A substantial, and not a literal, compliance is all that the law requires, and a recitation that the parties acknowledged that they executed the same as their free act and deed, without the words "and voluntary," does not affect the validity of the acknowledgment. Mosier v. Momsen, 13 Ok. 41, 74 P. 905.

The statute does not provide for the attestation of the written election in any other form than acknowledgment. Hence a written election witnessed, but not acknowledged, is insufficient to secure filing.

Other statutes which should be kept in mind when preparing a written election are:

ric "No instrument affecting the title to real estate shall be filed for record or recorded unless plainly printed or written or partly printed and partly written in the English language." (Sec. 9691,

O. S. 1931, Sec. 28, O. S. A., Title 16.) 

"When real estate is conveyed or encumbered by an instrument in writing by a person who cannot write his name, he shall execute the same by his mark, and his name shall be written near such mark by one or two persons who saw such mark made, who shall write their names on such instrument as witnesses. In case such instrument is acknowledged, then the officer taking the acknowledgment shall, in addition to the other necessary recitals in the acknowledgment, state that the grantor executed the instrument, by inserting in the ordinary form of acknowledgment by individuals after the words 'foregoing instrument' the word 'by his mark, in my presence and in the presence of and as witnesses.'" Sec. 9703, O. S. 1931, Sec. 34, O. S. A., Title 16.) "All instruments affecting real estate and executed and acknowledged in substantial compliance herewith shall be received in evidence in all courts without further proof of their execution; and in all cases where copies or other instruments might lawfully be used in evidence, copies of the same, duly certified from the records by the register of deeds may be received in evidence; and if the same need not be recorded to be valid for the purpose for which such evidence is offered, a copy duly verified by oath or affidavit of any person knowing the same to be a true copy, may be received in evidence." (Sec. 

1170, O. S. 1931, Sec. 27, O. S. A., Title 16.)

Section 11-Validity of Election. 

The written election is subject to all the requirements as to its validity as any other written instrument and may be attacked on the same ground and for the same reasons as other instruments affecting the title to property. If it has been obtained by fraud, duress or coercion, by undue influence or in any manner going to the validity of its execution or delivery, it is open to the same attacks and under like actions as other transactions.

Section 12-Marriage. 

Section 1 of the act provides: "This Act shall be available only to and apply only to husbands and wives.

" Hence, parties

to an election must be husband and wife. Under this section arises the question: When are a man and woman legally married under the statutes and decisions of this State? 

No question can arise where a man and woman have been married under a license duly issued and by a ceremony prescribed by the statutes. It is only where a man and woman have been living together without the form of statutory marriage that the problem arises as to whether or not they are husband and wife under the meaning of the phrase used in this 

Act. 

A common law marriage exists where competent parties agree to be and become immediately man and wife, and pursuant thereto enter into and maintain thereafter the marriage relation. Marriage in the legal sense is a civil contract, and it is not indispensable that a clergyman should be present to authorize and confirm the contract in order to give validity to the marriage. Statutes regulating marriage are usually directory merely, and when such statutes do not expressly prohibit or forbid other forms of marriage, a common law marriage, consummated in accordance with the rules of the common law, is valid. The general rule is that statutes which direct that a license must be procured, and that only certain persons shall perform the ceremony, that a certain number of witnesses shall be present, and that a certificate of the marriage shall be signed, returned and recorded, and that persons violating the conditions shall be guilty of a criminal offense, are directory merely, being addressed to persons in authority to secure publicity and a record of marriage, and will in no wise affect the validity of the marriage contract unless they contain an express provision to that effect. A common law marriage is valid. In Re Love's 

Estate, Love v. Love, 42 Ok. 478, 142 P. 305.

To establish common law marriage, there must be at least mutual agreement, consent or intention, which, however may be implied from conduct of parties to become man and wife, followed by cohabitation as such. In Re Graham's Estate, 169 

Ok. 568, 37 P. (2d) 964. All that is necessary to render competent parties, husband and wife, is agreement in present tense to be such. Tiuna v. Willmott, 162 Ok. 42, 19 P. (2d) 145.

Where a man and wife were formally married, at a time when the latter was under a disability because a decree of

943 divorce granted her had not become final by the lapse of six months, and continued to live together after the disability was removed in good faith without a formal marriage ceremony, such was sufficient to give rise to a common law marriage. 

Mudd v. Perry, 108 Ok. 168, 235 P. 479.

Remarriage to each other of a divorced couple may be shown by facts from which a common law marriage may be presumed. Thomas v. James, 69 Ok. 285, 171 P. 855.

Sections 1677 and 1678, O. S. 1931, Sections 12 and 13, Title 

43, O. S. A. (Miscegenation Statutes), are intended to apply to all persons, resident or otherwise, in this State, and are intended to prohibit marriage of the descendants of the African race with any other race. Under the provisions of the above statutes, persons domiciled in this State cannot evade the inhibition of the law by going to another state and there marrying and then returning to this State to reside and have such marriage recognized by the law of this state. Such marriage is void and confers no rights of persons or property. A prohibited marriage under state law may be inquired into in any proceeding in which the fact of marriage may be material. The statutes of the various states define marriage as a status fixed by civil contract, and our courts have held that because of the far-reaching results of this status upon the body politic, the contract must be construed in its relation to society and the state. The parties to the marriage cannot shun the burdens resulting from their illegal acts, neither can they profit by the illegal contract. 

For a resident or citizen of this state, marriage, under Section 

1677, O. S. 1931, Section 12, Title 43, O. S. A., is void whether contracted in this state or out of this state, because the statute forbids it and it is impossible of ratification by time or change or conduct of the parties and no rights of person or property are conferred by such marriage. Eggers, et al., v. Olson, et al., 104 Ok. 297, 231 P. 483.

Questions arising out of divorce and separation agreements between a man and his wife and their effect upon community property will be discussed in full in another chapter of this book. 

It is the opinion of the authors that a man and woman signing, acknowledging and filing a written election, whatever the status of their relation prior to such acts, will fix their relations thereafter as man and wife in so far as community property

is concerned unless their relation violates some statute strictly forbidding their marriage. The courts will probably construe such act as a solemn and public admission of their marriage without other evidence.

Section 13-Married Person Acting as Single Person. 

If either spouse sees fit to allow the other to act and represent himself or herself as a single person, under such circumstances and for such a time as would induce persons of reasonable prudence to believe that such spouse is in fact a single person, then the community has no existence as far as the public, having no knowledge of the legal relation of husband and wife, are concerned, and neither the community, nor either member thereof, can assert its existence to the detriment of those dealing with such spouse as a single person. Sadler v. 

Niesz, 5 Wash. 182, 31 P. 630.

Where a wife has parted from her husband and has treated such separation as final, permitting him to go where he pleased and do what he liked without any effort to follow him, during which separation he has held himself out as a single person, and has acquired and conveyed lands as an unmarried man, the wife is estopped from claiming a community interest in such lands as against an innocent purchaser. Nuhn v. Miller, 5 Wash. 405, 31 P. 1031.

Whether or not a married man or his wife, after having filed an election, could afterwards hold themselves out as single persons and thus come within the rule of the above cases, is doubtful in Oklahoma. The burden would probably rest upon a person relying on their representations to ascertain whether or not they had filed an election, or were divorced.

Chapter Three SEPARATE PROPERTY Section 14-Time When Community Sets Up. 

On the first day of the subsequent month following the date of the filing of the written election as explained in Chapter Two, the community property system sets up. Thereafter all property, both real and personal, acquired by both the husband and wife, is divided into separate and community property. Under the definition heretofore given, all property which is not separate property, becomes community property. 

Sections 3, 4, 5 and 6, Article 2, Chapter 62, S. L. 1939, reads as follows: 

"All property, both real and personal, of the husband owned or claimed by him before the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act, as provided in Section 1 of this Act, and that acquired afterwards by gift, including gifts of the wife's interest in community property, by division of community property, by devise, or by descent, as also the increase of all lands thus owned or acquired, shall be his separate property. The separate property of the husband shall not be subject to the debts contracted by the wife or liable for her torts, either before or after the effective date of said election, except as may be permitted by law as to his property prior to the enactment of this Act. The husband shall have the sole management, control and disposition of his separate property, both real and personal, to the extent permitted by law as to his property prior to the enactment of this Act." (Sec. 3, Art. 2, Ch. 62, S. L. 1939) 

"All property, both real and personal, of the wife owned or claimed by her before the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act as provided in Section 1 of this Act, and that acquired afterwards by gift, including gifts of the husband's interest in community property, by division of community property, by devise, or by descent, as also the increase of all lands thus owned or acquired, shall be her separate

property. The separate property of the wife shall not be subject to the debts contracted by the husband or liable for his torts, either before or after the effective date of said election, except as may be permitted by law as to her property prior to the enactment of this Act. The wife shall have the sole management, control and disposition of her separate property, both real and personal, to the extent permitted by law to her property prior to the enactment of this Act." (Sec. 4, Art. 2, Ch. 62, S. L. 1939) "All property or money received as compensation for personal injuries sustained either by the husband or the wife shall be the separate property of the person sustaining such injuries." 

(Sec. 5, Art. 2, Ch. 62, S. L. 1939) 

"All property acquired by the husband or the wife after the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act as provided in Section 1 of this Act, except that which is separate property of either one or the other, shall be deemed the community or common property of the husband and the wife and each, subject to the provisions of this Act, shall be vested with an undivided one-half interest therein. The wife shall have the management and control and may dispose of that portion of the community property consisting of her earnings, all rents, interest, dividends, income and other profits for her separate estate and all other community property the title to which stands in her name. The husband shall have the management and control and may dispose of all other community property, provided, however, that the homestead, if community property, shall not be sold, encumbered, or otherwise disposed of, except nanner as is provided by law prior to the enactment of this Act, and further provided, that any funds on deposit in any bank or banking institution, whether in the name of the husband or the wife, shall be presumed to be the separate property of the party in whose name they stand, regardless of who made the deposit, and unless said bank or banking institution is notified to the contrary, it shall be governed accordingly in honoring checks and orders against such account." (Sec. 6,

Art. 2, Ch. 62, S. L. 1939) Section 15-What Constitutes Separate Property. 

An examination of Sections 3 and 4 discloses that all property of both spouses, which was separate property prior to the effective date of election, together with all property thereafter

acquired by gift, descent, devise, or by division of community property, continues as separate property after the inception of the community. Property acquired by gift includes gifts of property between husband and wife of community property. 

The phrase, "the increase of all lands thus owned or acquired" presents some difficulties of interpretation. We shall return to it later.

The section of the Act which fixes the community status of property is Section 6, part of which reads as follows: 

"All property acquired by the husband or the wife after the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act as provided in Section 1 of this Act, except that which is separate property of either one or the other, shall be deemed the community or common property of the husband and wife ✶ ✶ ✶” 

Section 16-Status of After Acquired Property. 

This covers all property acquired after the first of the month subsequent to the effective date of the written election. It includes all types of property, both real and personal, as well as the income, rents, earnings, dividends, and profits arising out of separately owned property. From this point on property acquired by either spouse flows into and becomes a part of the community, except that acquired by gift, descent, devise, and as provided by the phrase "the increase of all lands thus. owned or acquired."

Section 17-Gifts. 

Some of the nicest problems to be solved under community property arise out of the question whether after acquired property is separate or community. No rigid rule can be given as a guide. It is clear that any property coming to either husband or wife as a gift, through descent or devise, is, under the Act, the separate property of the person to whom given, devised or vested by descent. The question arises out of property claimed as a gift. A gift of property to both the husband and wife is a gift to the community. A gift of community property by one spouse to the other divests it of community status and changes it to separate property.

Where there has been a conveyance of property between either of the spouses it is sometimes necessary to determine the intent of the parties. The acts and declarations of the husband before taking the conveyance, having reference to it,

and corresponding with his after acts, evidencing his intentions and purposes respecting it, and the subsequent statements of the wife, in so far as they conduced to countervail the prima facie inference deducible from the fact of taking the deed in her name, were admissible and proper to be submitted to the jury for their consideration in coming to a conclusion as to what was the real purpose in taking the deed in the wife's name, whether or not to make a gift of such property to her separate estate. Smith v. Strahan, 25 Tex. 103.

Section 18-Inventory of Wife's Separate Property. 

The Oklahoma statute covering the holding of separate property by the wife is Section 1658, O. S. 1931.

In this State a married woman occupies the same status as her husband with respect to the right to contract, maintain, and defend actions, engage in business and acquire and convey property. Title to her property is not clouded with any presumption that such property was acquired by the efforts or means of her husband. The wife's separate property cannot be subjected to the payment of her husband's debt; but if, with her knowledge and consent, he is permitted to deal with it as his own and obtain credit upon it, such acts on her part amount to an estoppel in pais. For such estoppel to be effective in favor of a creditor of her husband in an action to subject her property to the payment of her husband's debts, the facts showing such estoppel must be specifically pleaded, and must be established by the weight of the evidence. At a time when the husband is not indebted, he may make a gift of the property to his wife. Such gift is valid, and vests the title to such property in his wife. When a husband acquires the legal title to land belonging to his wife's separate estate, he is deemed to hold it in trust for her benefit, in the absence of direct evidence that she intended to make a gift of the property to him. When irreconcilable differences arise between the husband and wife, a conveyance by him of property to her for the protection and support of herself and their children, in consideration of articles of separation, is valid, and vests title in her as against creditors of the husband not holding a lien against such property. Farmers' State Bank of Ada v. Keen, et al., 66 Ok. 62, 167 P. 207.

The rule is, where a husband purchases land with his own. money and takes title thereto in the name of his wife, or in the joint name of himself and wife, no trust arises in favor of the

husband, by reason thereof, in the lands standing in the name of the wife, but the presumption of the law is, in the absence of clear and convincing evidence to the contrary, that an advancement or gift was intended. This is so because in the law the legal obligation rests upon the husband to support the wife. 

A husband has the right to convey land to his wife or to have it conveyed to her, either as a gift outright or in payment of a debt owed to her, and in the absence of fraud or interests of creditors, the presumption of law is in favor of such conveyance. 

Where a husband places improvements on the wife's land, they are presumed to be placed there as a gift to the wife. Yates, Admr., et al., v. Yates, 93 Ok. 94, 219 P. 705.

A married man, during his lifetime, may give away his separate property, and such gift will be valid and binding against his widow, where she is not a creditor within the contemplation of the statute against fraudulent conveyances. Garrison v. 

Spencer, et al., 58 Ok. 442, 160 P. 493. See also Farrell v. 

Puthoff, 13 Ok. 159, 74 P. 96.

A husband has the right to convey land to his wife or to have it conveyed to her, either as a gift outright or in payment of a debt owed to her, and in the absence of fraud or interests of creditors, the presumption of law is in favor of such conveyance. Where a husband is entitled to the reconveyance of the title to land, and expresses a desire to the reconveyancor that the deed of reconveyance be made to his wife, and in compliance with his desire, and at his special request, such deed is made to his wife, she thereby acquires title to the land, and a deed from her vests title in her grantees. Kent, et al., v. Tallent, et al., 75 Ok. 185, 183 P. 422. 

Where by statute, as in this jurisdiction, a married woman may dispose of her separate estate by deed the same as if she were a femme sole, the discharge of her husband's debt constitutes sufficient consideration to support the wife's conveyance of her separate real estate made for that purpose. Thomas v. 

Halsell, 63 Ok. 203, 164 P. 458.

A conveyance of community property from the husband to the wife changes its character from community to the separate property of the wife. Main v. Main, 7 Ariz. 149, 60 P. 888.

Money saved by a wife from the funds given her by her husband for household expenses does not thereby lose its community

character and become her separate property. Abbott v. Wetherly, 6 Wash. 507, 33 P. 1070. The taking possession by a wife with the consent of the husband as her separate property of articles purchased with money which she has earned, and which it has previously been agreed between them shall be her separate property, constitutes a gift of said articles to her as her separate property. Yake v. Pugh, 13 Wash. 78, 42 P. 528.

The action of the husband in consummating a trade for certain property, taking the deed thereof in his wife's name and delivering same to the wife as a gift is sufficient, as between grantor and grantee, to establish his intention to have the wife take the property as her separate estate. Nixon v. Post, 13 

Wash. 181, 43 P. 23.

A donation in remuneration for or in compensation for services rendered by a married woman to the donor is not a part of the community estate. Fisk v. Flores, 43 Tex. 340. A pension paid to a veteran is a donation from the government and is his separate property, and the fact that he invested it in real property does not change its character into community property. 

Johnson v. Johnson, (Tex.) 23 S. W. 1022.

Community property in existence or as it comes into existence, may be made the separate property of the wife by gift from the husband. Kellett v. Trice, 95 Tex. 160, 66 S. W. 51.

Where a father gives certain lands to his daughter and she and her husband enter into possession and make permanent improvements, the equitable title to the property is vested in the donee in her separate right. Cauble v. Worsham, 96 Tex. 86, 70 S. 

W. 737.

Real or personal common property given by the husband to his wife becomes her separate property, and will not be liable for debts by him afterward contracted. Peck v. Brummagin, 31 Cal. 441. In dealings between husband and wife, the intention of the parties is of paramount importance, where the question as to the effect of a conveyance of real estate arises between themselves; and where it appears that a conveyance to the wife was intended, as between the husband and wife, to vest the title to the property in her as her separate estate, the courts will respect their intention, and, as between themselves, will uphold her title to the property, although the legal effect of the purchase and conveyance would, independent of such intention,

make the property community. Flournoy v. Flournoy, 86 Cal. 

286, 24 P. 1012.

A husband and wife may hold real or personal property together as joint tenants, or tenants in common. The wife may, without the consent of her husband, convey her separate property. A full and complete inventory of the separate personal property of the wife may be made out and signed by her, acknowledged or proved in the manner provided by law for the acknowledgment or proof of a grant of real property by an unmarried woman, and recorded in the office of the register of deeds of the county in which the parties reside. The filing of the inventory in the register's office is notice and prima facie evidence of the title of the wife. 

The failure of a wife to file a full and complete inventory of her separate personal property as provided for in Section 

1658, O. S. 1931, does not estop her right to assert ownership in the same, nor does such failure give the husband the right to dispose of the property by mortgage or otherwise. Under the statute a married woman has a right to acquire property in her own name, hold and dispose of the same as if unmarried, and her separate property is not liable for the debts of her husband; neither can he sell or encumber the same, and there is no presumption arising from the marriage relation of ownership of the separate property of the wife in favor of the husband. Caylor Lumber Co. v. Mays, et al., 73 Ok. 30, 174 P. 521.

Section 19-Division of Community Property. 

In Sections 3 and 4 of the Community Property Act, describing what constitutes separate property, occurs the phrase "the division of community property." The meaning of this language, taken in connection with the entire Act, is not clear. 

Gifts from each to the other by husband and wife are covered by another phrase, and division of property upon divorce is given as one method of dissolving the community. These words have not appeared in the statutes of any other community property state, and are without construction in reference to community property law. It would appear, therefore, that they refer only to conveyances made between a husband and wife for the purpose of cutting up the community property into separate property under situations which could not be classified as gifts. 

Inasmuch as the Act specifically provides only two methods of dissolving the community, to-wit: divorce or death, it is

not believed that this phrase furnishes another method of dissolving the community. Yet on the other hand, the language of these sections lend color to the notion that the community might be dissolved by a division of all of the community property between the husband and wife by mutual conveyances so as to take out of the community all of the community property existing at the time of the conveyances, but if the community in Oklahoma only takes in after acquired property together with the income from separately owned property, it appears more logical to assume that the phrase above referred to does not offer another method of dissolving the community. 

Section 20-Devise and Descent. 

Under this phrase falls all property received by either husband or wife, after their written election, through inheritance and under descent and distribution. No extended discussion of this method of acquiring separate property is required. Property, both real and personal, inherited by either husband or wife, after the community has been set up, constitutes separate property in the hands of the one receiving it and is not affected by the community property law. The same rule applies in all of the other community property states. 

Section 21-Increase of Lands. 

The words "increase of lands thus owned or acquired" used in both Sections 3 and 4 of the Act appear only in the statutes of the State of Texas in describing what constitutes the separate property of the husband and wife. The legal meaning of this term has not been either definitely or clearly determined, although in a great many cases it has been defined in a negative way. In the early case of De Blane v. Hugh Lynch & Co., 23 Tex. 25, the court in construing these words said: "In an etymological sense, it cannot be doubted, that the word 'increase' as applied to land, means that which grows out of it, or is produced by its cultivation; but to adopt this meaning of the word, and to interpret the statute accordingly, would lead to results inequitable and unreasonable, and wholly inconsistent with the recognized principles of law upon which the system of community property is based-that whatever is acquired by the joint efforts of the husband and wife, shall be their common property."

It was also held in the case of Craxton, Wood & Co. v. Ryan,

VA^A^A MN et al., 3 Tex. Ct. App. 367, that brick made on land which was separate property of the wife, and out of the dirt of said land, where the husband had nothing to do with the making of them and paid no part of the expenses, were community property. 

In a later case, Cabell v. Menczer, et al., 35 S. W. 206, the 

Texas Court of Civil Appeals used the following language: "It is well settled by our supreme court that the proceeds arising from the sale or exchange of separate property of the spouses becomes the separate property of such spouse whose property is sold or exchanged. Rose v. Houston, 11 Tex. 324; Chapman v. Allen, 15 Tex. 278; Bank v. Weems, 69 Tex. 499, 6 S. W. 802; 

Smith v. Bailey, 66 Tex. 553, 1 S. W. 627. It has also been held that crops grown on the separate land of either husband or wife become community property. Conner v. Hawkins, 66 Tex. 639, 2 S. W. 520; Cleveland v. Cole, 65 Tex. 402. Interest accruing on money, the separate property of the wife, is community property. Braden v. Gose, 57 Tex. 37. Profits arising from the investment of money in a mercantile business is community property. Epperson v. Jones, 65 Tex. 425; Smith v. Bailey, 66 

Tex. 553, 1 S. W. 627. While these principles are well established by our decisions, we have been unable to find any decision of our supreme court, nor have counsel for appellants cited any, which goes to the extent of holding, as contended for, that the profits made in the sale or exchange of land in the manner as shown by the facts in this case, become community property. 

When the separate property is invested in real estate, and the same is sold at a profit, and the proceeds arising therefrom are reinvested in real estate, which is also resold, the same is separate property; and it is immaterial how often mutations take place, as long as its changed condition can be traced and identified by clear and satisfactory proof. The term 'increase of land,' as used in the statute, evidently must include the profits arising from the sale of land. If not, it is meaningless." 

In the same year that the Texas Court handed down the case of Cabell v. Menczer, et al., cited above, it held in Evans v. Purinton, et al., 34 S. W. 350, as follows: "By our law, the increase of all land the separate property of either of the spouses (unlike the increase of money or other personal property) remains the separate property of that spouse, Sayles' Civ. 

St. art. 2851. In this case it must be regarded as uncontroverted that the land standing in the name of M. H. Purinton, the

mother, at the death of the latter, became, by devise, the separate property of the wife, M. M. Purinton. It became, then, the duty of the husband, as such, to manage this separate property. If one of the tracts thus acquired increased in a year 

50 per cent in value, and through the negotiations of the husband, charged by law with the management of the property, was sold by the wife, joined by her husband, at a profit thus estimated, this profit would but represent the increase of the land; and a second tract, purchased with the proceeds of the first, representing the value thereof, measured by its original value plus the profits at the date of the sale, would, we think, be impressed with the character of separate property. This view is not believed to be in conflict with the rule of our supreme court established in De Blane v. Lynch, 23 Tex. 25, and in the numerous cases following it. In that case, in stating the doctrine to be that crops raised on land the separate property of the wife, by the labor of slaves also her separate property, are community property of the husband and wife, the court uses the following language: 'If it be admitted that the "increase of land" meant by our statute is the product of the soil itself, then it would follow that a crop grown on the land of her husband, by the labor of the slaves of the wife, would be the separate property of the husband, because it was the "increase" of his land. The husband has, by the statute, the control and management of the separate property of the wife; and if the husband owned land, as separate property, and the wife owned slaves, as separate property, the husband could always employ the wife's slaves in the cultivation of his own land, and thus add to his separate property by the use of her separate property.' It will be thus noted that the foundation of the doctrine there held is the protection of the wife's separate estate. 

Here the application of the rule to the increased value of the wife's land represented by the profits realized from its sale, and reinvested in other land, taken in her name, would result, not in the protection of the separate property, but in its loss, to the extent, at least, of the increase of her land represented by the profits accruing from the sale, and applied in reinvestments. 

It will be further noted that the increase in the value of these lands is to be ascribed rather to the operation of the laws of trade, due to the growth and prosperity of the country, than to the labors of the husband. These labors consisted only in the negotiations of the sales, and in the embracing with watchful eye of such opportunities as presented themselves for profit- 40

able sales of the wife's estate, and its profitable reinvestment. 

Such services seem to us to be reasonably within the scope of the duty charged by law upon the husband of managing the wife's property. In our opinion, the rendition of such services cannot be said to impart a community character to the property thus acquired, because the labor performed in rendering them is but an incident to the sole management of the wife's separate property, incumbent, under the law, upon the husband. A., a penniless husband, discovers after his marriage with B., the wife, that the latter owns in her own separate right wild lands of the value of $5,000. In the course of a year, owing to the demand for land, due to the impulse of a growing population seeking homes, it grows in value to the extent of $10,000. Can creditors complain that the insolvent husband should discover purchasers for this land, or any part of a particular tract thereof; that he should negotiate its sale, and, by means of the conveyance by his wife, reinvest in other lands the profits thus realized? We think not. The husband's business under such circumstances is but the management of the wife's separate property, in the discharge of a duty which under the law he owes to her and to her property."

Section 22-Jointly Owned Property. 

While the Community Property Act does not speak of the jointly owned property of husband and wife either before or after the written election, except in that section of the Act which provides for the control of each over their property and the community property (providing that the wife has control over all community property standing in her name; the husband control over all other community property), these sections under the circumstances can refer only to after acquired property and not to property owned by either or both of the spouses previous to the filing of the election. As the Act stands, jointly owned property, whether under the form of joint tenancy or tenancy in common, will remain outside the community with the same status as before except as to its rents and profits, if owned prior to the inception of the community. It is neither separate nor community property in a precise sense, except as to the undetermined separate interest of the husband and wife, which may be called separate property. It would follow from this that any change of such jointly owned property after the commencement of the community would not affect the status of such property unless the husband and wife clearly intended

to convert it from separate property into community property. 

As another chapter of this book is devoted to a discussion of transmutation of property into the community, the problem will not be here discussed further. 

If the situation should arise where property has heretofore been deeded or may be deeded, to a married person after the filing of an election in such fashion as to attempt to reserve the rents and profits from such land to either the husband or wife as their separate property, it is very probable that regardless of the consideration paid for the land, our court will hold such a reservation as in the nature of gift or devise so as to give it the status of separate property in so far as the rents and income are concerned. However, in this situation, it must be remembered that the Oklahoma community consists practically of rents and income, and not of real property. Therefore, it will be logical for the court to hold that such a reservation is of no avail to keep said rents and income out of the community. 

Section 23-Damages for Personal Injuries. 

Under the provisions of Section 5 of the Act, damages recovered for personal injuries are the separate property of the husband or wife respectively. "All property or money received as compensation for personal injuries sustained either by the husband or the wife shall be the separate property of the person sustaining such injuries." 

Section 24-Estoppel. 

Where a married woman places her husband in charge of her business as manager, permits him to buy and sell, incur and pay bills, and he obtains credit upon statement that he owns business, and his wife does not notify creditor to the contrary she is estopped as against said creditor to claim business as her separate property. Boise Butcher Co. v. Anixdale, 26 Idaho 483, 144 P. 337.

Where the husband procures credit upon a financial statement of his assets, among which he lists his wife's real estate standing in his name, wife cannot claim title to such property as against creditors of her husband. Chaney v. Gould Co., 28 

Idaho 76, 152 P. 468.

Section 25-Earnings of Wife Living Separate from Husband. 

Section 1659, O. S. 1931, Paragraph 2, reads as follows:

IC The earnings of the wife are not liable for the debts of the husband, and the earnings and accumulations of the wife, and of her minor children living with her or in her custody, while she is living separate from her husband, are the separate property of the wife.")

Under a statute providing that accumulations of a wife while she is living separate from her husband are her separate property, a husband's land placed by him in his wife's possession and acquired by her while living separate from him is an accumulation, and is not subject to execution for the husband's debts. Union Oil Co. v. Stewart, et al., 158 Cal. 149, 110 P. 313.

Section 26-Insurance. 

One of the perplexing questions certain to arise under the Community Property Law is the status of insurance; life, accident, endowment and other forms. The decisions of other community property states are divided on this subject and the most that can be done is to discuss this matter from two angles-the angle in which insurance under most circumstances is held to be separate property, and from the other angle holding it in many instances to be community property. 

Our community property statute contains no provision which by interpretation would fix the status of insurance. Therefore, before construing any of the decisions of other states in reference to whether life insurance or the proceeds thereof is community property or belongs to the separate estate of the beneficiary, attention is called to the following sections of the Oklahoma statutes: "Every policy of life insurance made payable to or for the benefit of a married woman, or which after its issue is assigned, transferred, or in any way made payable to a married woman, or to any person in trust for her or for her benefit, whether procured by herself, her husband or by any other person and whether the assignment or transfer is made by her husband or by any other person, shall inure to her separate use and benefit, subject to the provisions of the preceding section, relating to premiums paid in fraud of creditors, and subject to any indebtedness on account of the policy: Provided, that the insured under such policy shall not be denied the right to change

the beneficiary where this right is expressly reserved in the policy." (Section 10518, O. S. 1931) 

"When a policy of insurance is effected by any person on his own life, or on another life in favor of some person other than himself having an insurable interest therein, the lawful beneficiary thereof, other than himself or his legal representative shall be entitled to its proceeds against the creditors and representatives of the person effecting the same; and the person to whom a policy of life insurance, is made payable, may maintain an action thereon in his own name: Provided, that subject to the statute of limitations, the amount of any premiums for said insurance paid in fraud of creditors with interest thereon shall inure to their benefit from the proceeds of the policy; but the company issuing the policy shall be discharged of all liability thereon by payment of its proceeds in accordance with the terms thereof, unless, before such payment, the company shall have written notice by or in behalf of some creditor, with specifications of the amount claimed, claiming to recover for certain premiums paid in fraud of creditors; Provided, that the insured, under such policy shall not be denied the right to change the beneficiary when such right is expressly reserved in the policy." (Section 10517, O. S. 1931) "The money or other benefit, charity, relief or aid to be paid, provided or rendered by any association authorized to do business under this article, shall not be liable to attachment, by trustee, garnishee or other process, and shall not be seized, taken, appropriated or applied by any legal or equitable process, or by operation of law, to pay any debt or liability of the certificate holder, or of any beneficiary named in any certificate, or any person who may have any right thereunder." (Section 10568, O. S. 1931)

It would seem from the language of the above sections that the proceeds of a life insurance policy payable to a married woman will not be held to be community property under any circumstances and under all types of policies. For this reason the following decisions must be construed in connection with the above quoted sections. 

In the State of Washington life insurance or the proceeds thereof are not mere expectancies or choses in action, but are "property", and, if premiums are paid from assets of community,

insurance constitutes "community property." Occidental Life 

Ins. Co. v. Powers, 74 P. (2d) 27. In the opinion this case further held: "The salient facts in this action are admitted by the parties vitally interested and may be briefly summarized as follows: That on July 12, 1911, Corinne Leone

Powers and Leon Frank Powers were married and continued to be husband and wife until the death of Mr. Powers on August 

10th, 1935; that on August 15th, 1931, the American Medical

Life Company, (which was afterwards succeeded by the plaintiff, The Occidental Life Insurance Company) executed and delivered to Mr. Powers its policy of life insurance, Exhibit No. 

9, whereby it agreed to pay the beneficiary named therein, viz., Corinne Leone Powers, his wife, the sum of $5,000.00 upon the death of the insured; that the policy contained a clause giving the insured the right to name a new beneficiary at any time during the continuance of the policy; that the policy also contained a provision wherein it was stipulated that the insured could receive every benefit, exercise every right conferred by the policy without the consent of the beneficiary; that the original cost and all the premiums on the policy were paid from community funds; that on December 5th, 1934, the insured in proper form, without the knowledge or consent of his wife, designated new beneficiaries, viz., Minnie Lombard Powers, his mother; and Fern Marie Safford, his private secretary, onehalf to each and these were the beneficiaries designated in the policy at the time of Mr. Powers' death.

After his death, Mrs. Powers, the wife; Minnie Lombard 

Powers, the mother, and Fern Marie Safford, each made claim to the proceeds of the policy and as a result of the conflicting claims the insurance company, under the interpleader statutes, paid the amount due into court and it thereby becomes its duty to determine the rights of claimants, thereto. 

It is the contention of Mrs. Powers, the wife, that the whole sum must be awarded to her for the reason that the policy being community property, her husband could not give it or its proceeds away without her consent. In other words, she contends that without adequate consideration to the community therefor, her husband could not, without her consent, make a change of beneficiary. In addition, it is also her claim that the change of beneficiary on the part of the insured was

in effect an attempt to make a testamentary disposition of their community property and not having the legal requirements of a will, it was, therefore, void.

The community property system and the rights of the spouses in property, both real and personal, acquired during the marital relation have been settled with reasonable certainty in this state. In Marston v. Rue, 92 Wash. 129, 159 P. 111, our supreme court uses this language: 'Now a wife's rights in family personalty are not of the contingent sort, like dower or survivorship, but a present estate. 

True, by our statute, the husband is made manager with full power to sell and dispose of this. But it does not follow that he can give it away. He is, so to speak, only the head of a firm. 

The personal property is just as much hers as his. The very statute that gives him sole power over the whole restricts his testamentary power to a half. Under our law she has helped to create it as much as he. Consequently, the idea is not to be tolerated that a husband can give away stocks and bonds or precious stones out of the family money. No part of those savings can he make gifts of against her consent, even to relatives, though mere trifles to the latter no doubt might be sustained under the rule of de minimis. The law cannot countenance his right to a wilful, premeditated waste of family personal property, which is now so often the bulk of an estate.' 

This decision was later amplified in Schramm v. Steele, 97 

Wash. 309, 166 P. 634, where it is said: "The husband is made, by statute, the manager, not the owner. His management and control include the power of absolute disposition, but only for the community. Else there is no such thing as a vested property right in the community as to any personal property, since the husband could give away all such property in any manner he pleased, except by will, at any time during the existence of the community. To hold that the whole substance of the term "community property" as applied to personalty consists in a mere contingent expectancy of the wife, would make of the term "community personal property," a palpable misnomer. It would take away every community element except the fact that a wife's labors and sacrifices had helped to earn it. It would destroy that equality which it is the obvious purpose of our community property law to conserve.'

See, also, Parker v. Parker, 121 Wash. 24, 207 P. 1062; 

Nimey v. Nimey, 182 Wash. 194, 45 P. (2d) 949; Johnston v. 

Johnston, 182 Wash. 573, 47 P. (2d) 1048; In re McGovern's 

Estate, 181 Wash. 231, 42 P. (2d) 796, 46 P. (2d) 1118.

The substance of these decisions is: 1. That the wife has a vested interest in community personal property. 2. That while the husband has the management and control of the community personal property he is only a statutory agent of the community, and 3. That a husband does not have the power to make substantial gifts of community personal property without the consent of his wife.

It is self-evident that a policy of life insurance upon the life of either spouse, resident of this state, when their community rights are to be determined, must necessarily be construed by the law of this state affecting such rights. That the policy in question is a community asset cannot be seriously disputed. The marital relation existed; the insurance was upon the life of the husband; the beneficiary was the wife; and the premiums were paid from the community earnings. It is true that the policy is in the form of an endowment, but this feature rather augments than detracts from the holding that it is community personal property.

The policy represents the investments of community funds for the benefit of the wife and family, effective when and if the community is deprived of the earnings and support of the husband and father. But if the husband shall be living at the end of the endowment period and the policy is in force, the proceeds of its investment will be returned to the community. 

In other words, while this policy did not have all the attributes of an investment in stocks or bonds it did represent value in cash, present and future.

Holding that a policy is property see May v. Rudell, 149 

Wash. 393, at page 398, 270 P. 1041.

What power, then, has the husband as statutory agent of the community over the policy?
