No creditor shall have recourse to the community property for the payment of debts or liabilities created by either the husband or the wife, except as provided in Section 7 of this Act; provided, however, that any creditor may satisfy his claim or demand out of the community property which was under the management, control and disposition of the spouse incurring the indebtedness or liability at the time the debt or liability was contracted or created, and which has been subsequently conveyed or transferred to the other spouse and is under the management, control and disposition of said other spouse, without proof that said creditor relied upon said community property in advancing said credit, but without prejudice to the rights of the third party purchasers, incumbrancers, or other creditors or grantees; and provided further, that the husband or wife on paying community debts shall, as between themselves, charge the same against community property." 

Section 48-Separate Debts. 

Sections 3 and 4 make no change in the law in reference to the liability of a husband or wife for their separate debts, leaving the law in this respect after an election the same as it is without an election.

Section 7 burdens that portion of the community property standing in the name of the wife with her separate debts and that portion standing in the name of the husband with his separate debts. Thus, in so far as community property is concerned, those portions remain subject to separate debts the same as separate property.

An analysis of Section 8 discloses that no creditor may have recourse to community property for the payment of his debt except as provided in Section 7, modified by permitting him to follow changes of property in fraud of his rights.

Under the plain language of these sections it is difficult to determine what may be "community debts" and what remedies a creditor has for their enforcement against community property. Nowhere in the Act does it clearly specify that a community debt must be paid out of community property, except after the death of one of the spouses, or that community property is liable therefor. 

The phrase, "community debts" is used in the Act at the end of Section 8, in the following sentence: "that the husband or wife on paying community debts shall, as between themselves, charge the same against community property," and in Section 15 in the words, "all debts of the Community." These do not define community debt, and unless it can be assumed that the Act providing for separate debts meant all other debts to be community debts, the Act as it stands is inadequate to protect creditors of the community, or those relying upon community property for payment. It will require legislative amendment to clarify the Act on this score, or some rather difficult interpretation by the Supreme Court. 

A creditor may subject the separate property of the wife, or the separate property of the husband, as the case may be, to the payment of their separate debts. He may also subject that portion of the community which stands in the name of his separate debtor, or which is under his or her control, or which has been conveyed to the other spouse after the creation of the debt.

Each member of the community owns an undivided one-half interest, which may or may not be under his or her control. 

A creditor can subject either half to process against the one creating the debt, if it is a separate debt. He apparently cannot levy against the other half under these sections. 

A husband and wife jointly own property acquired after the election. The husband creates a debt on behalf of the community. The creditor can proceed against the husband's half only unless the whole of such property stands in his name or has been placed under his control. The same situation arises on the same facts on a debt created by the wife. Hence, it would seem the community in its entirety cannot be subjected to debts except where the husband or wife jointly created or assumed the debt, or after the death of one of the spouses. 

It is clear that conflicting questions will arise around the

construction of these sections. For instance; is the wife the agent of the husband in the creation of indebtedness for necessities, the operation of the community, or arising out of emergencies so as to bind the property of husband, and vice versa? Can the husband incur community indebtedness other than that specified in the above sentence and bind the wife's interest in the community? If the community property system in Oklahoma should be held to be a type of partnership these questions will be resolved in one direction; if held to be an entity, then in still another direction. Until our Supreme 

Court or the legislature has either clarified these sections by interpretation or amendatory legislation, it is not possible to prophesy what the effect of these sections will be upon creditors. 

The following cases cited upon the rights of creditors and the debts and liability of the husband and wife and the community property must be weighed carefully in the light of what the above quoted sections provide. 

Wife's separate property cannot be subjected to payment of husband's debts. Farmers' State Bank of Ada v. Keen, et al., 66 Ok. 62, 167 P. 207.

If the husband neglects to make adequate provision for the support of his wife according to his circumstances, any other person may, in good faith supply her with articles necessary for her support and recover the reasonable value thereof from the husband, except where the wife has abandoned the husband, or is living separate from him, and there is no agreement for support and the abandonment is not justified. Schiefer v. 

Wilson, 171 Okl. 119, 42 P. (2d) 263.

In this same case the court gave a definition of necessaries for which the husband would be bound, as follows: "Necessaries are those things suitable to the rank and condition of the husband, or, in other words, to the condition of the parties in life. In a general way, whatever naturally and reasonably tends to relieve distress, or materially and in some essential particular is necessary to promote comfort, either of body or mind, may be deemed to be necessary." 

Where wife involuntarily supports herself when husband fails to, she may recover the amount so expended from him. 

Sodowsky v. Sodowsky, 51 Ok. 689, 152 P. 390.

133 Although it is the duty of the husband to make sufficient provisions for his wife, including a residence in which to live, according to his ability and their station in life, this fact does not prevent the wife from becoming personally liable for such provisions where she contracts with a third party for same and agrees to pay therefor. Millsap v. Kahn, 174 Ok. 158, 49 P. (2d) 729.

Part of Section 1653, O. S. 1931, provides: "The wife must support the husband when he has not deserted her out of her separate property when he has no separate property and he is unable from infirmity to support himself." 

The above section has apparently not been construed by the Oklahoma Supreme Court, but it is assumed that under a proper case, the court would hold the wife was in duty bound to support her husband to the same extent, and for the like necessaries where he is infirm and she has separate property, as he is bound to support her. 

The Community Property Law does not change this duty nor modify it as to either the husband or the wife. Therefore, it will be taken that debts created by either the husband or wife for necessaries contemplated by the above statutes and decisions will be treated by our court as the separate debt of the person properly charged therewith. However, it may hold that where necessaries have been furnished to either the husband or wife or their children, they may be properly charged against the community property, if any such property exists. 

This rule would only apply against the husband as the above statute is based upon the fact that the husband has no separate property.

Section 49-Agency. 

It will be presumed until our court holds otherwise that all rules of law applicable to agency as between the husband and wife in this State will not be changed in any particular by the Community Property Law, and that where agency exists either by reason of the actual appointment of the husband or wife to act as such by the other or by implication from their acts and conduct, the usual legal results will be the same regardless of whether the property dealt with under such agency is community property or separate property.

The law of notice under such circumstances will not be changed and what has heretofore been held to be notice to the husband or to the wife, as the case may be, in reference to transactions affecting either one or the marital relation, will not be affected by the existence of the community. However, this rule may be considerably changed if our court should take the position that the community is an entity separate and apart from the husband and wife, or is of the nature of a partnership, as the rules of agency and notice will be different under these theories than it has been heretofore in this State where only a husband and wife and the marital relation is concerned.

Thus, either the husband or the wife may nominate the other as the agent for the community with full power to control, manage, and sell under properly executed instruments granting such authority and such power of control may be given so as to include all of the community property, in which case, under the above sections of the Community Property Law, such community property would be liable for not only the debts of the individual spouse under whose control the property had been placed, but for all of what will be designated as community debts.

Under the Community Property Law the question of the extent of the wife's agency to bind the husband will arise in connection with community debts. Our court has held in the case of Baker v. Witten, 1 Ok. 160, 30 P. 491, that "In the absence of express authority, the husband is not liable for the wife's obligations, except for necessaries. The power of the wife to bind the husband on contract is based upon the grounds of agency." And has held in the case of Waken v. Davis, 112 

Okl. 23, 239 P. 659, that the relationship of husband and wife of itself does not authorize conclusion that husband is agent of wife. And, to the same effect that the relationship of husband and wife will not, unaccompanied by other circumstances, authorize the conclusion that the husband is the agent of the wife, but may be considered, and is usually entitled to considerable weight when taken in connection with other circumstances. Smith v. Cornwell & Chowning Lumber Co., 101 Ok. 

86, 223 P. 154; Drum v. Crews, 117 Ok. 92, 244 P. 774; Jones v. Exchange National Bank of Muskogee, 124 Okl. 5, 253 P. 49.

The question of the right of the husband to repudiate the debts and obligations created by his wife when living separate and apart from him, and his right to give public notice that he will not be bound for her debts is not treated herein, as it is not believed that these situations will affect any rule of community property.

Section 50-Definition of Community Debts. 

There has been no settled general rule as to what constitutes community debts. A broad definition of the term, "community debt," sufficient to cover any situation which may arise in is tate may be given as follows: A community debt is any debt for which the community, as distinguished from the husband. and wife, the community property, will be liable and consists of those debts which have arisen out of the transaction of the affairs of the community and which is not clearly the separate debt of either the husband or the wife. The difficulty of drawing a line in this State between separate debts and community debts grows out of the peculiar wording of our Community Property Law. Community debts must be those growing out of the transaction of business which clearly benefits the community, and which do not inure to the benefit of the separate property of the husband or wife. Of course, any debt jointly assumed by the husband and wife, in the absence of any indication to the contrary, will be a community debt in Oklahoma and subject the community property to the payment thereof. It follows that either the husband or the wife under our law can create community debts, provided such debts grow out of the management and control of community property, and which benefit the community property. Debts contracted in the ordinary course of business, which business is that of the community, must necessarily be community debts. 

In the case of Kidd v. Prince, 182 S. W. 725, the Civil Court of Appeals of Texas held that a surviving parent could not appropriate community funds to reimburse himself for money spent in the support and maintenance of his minor children, as this is in no sense a community debt. 

The same court in the case of Word v. Colley, 143 S. W. 

275, held that where a husband created a community debt and survived his wife, the power existed in the survivor to extend and renew a community obligation.

A debt incurred by the husband during the existence of the marriage relation is prima facie a debt of the community, and, in the absence of evidence to the contrary, a judgment for the debt will be deemed to have established it as a community debt. 

Dever v. Selz, (C. C. A. Tex.) 87 S. W. 891.

Every debt created by the husband during the existence of marriage is prima facie a community debt. Calhoun v. Leary, et al., 32 P. 1070. 6 Wash. 17.

Section 51-Family Expenses. 

It is not possible to give all of the decisions holding the community liable for certain debts. The most that can be done is to group the decisions under several heads and thus attempt to give a summarized outline of what the courts of other states have held on this subject. The following decisions have been grouped under the heads of family expense, bills and notes, liens, and guaranty and suretyship and torts, as being the most usual heads under which the searcher will find opinions on the subject of community debts.

A note given by a wife for necessaries for herself and family, and for expenses incurred for her separate property, executed by her alone or jointly with her husband, is valid, and can be enforced against either the common property or separate property of the wife. Hollis v. Francois, 5 Tex. 195, 51 Am. Dec. 760.

One who advances money to pay for the erection of a dwelling upon a lot owned by a married woman at a place to which she had gone for her health, is not entitled to a personal judgment against her, to be made out of her separate estate, upon the ground that the dwelling was necessary as a home for herself and children, although the family had previously lived in a rented house, there being no change of circumstances or conditions making it necessary to purchase property and erect a dwelling for a home at a place to which it does not appear that the husband had removed. Bexar Building and Loan Ass'n. v. Heady, 21 Tex. Civ. App. 154, 50 S. W. 1079.

Debt due from a wife for commissions for an exchange of her separate property is not for necessaries within Vernon's 

Sayles' Ann. Civ. St. 1914, Art. 4624, and community property is not liable therefor. Winkie v. Conatser, 171 S. W. 1017.

In the absence of any evidence showing a different practice, the community earnings of husband and wife are chargeable with the family support, and any amount expended for such purpose by either spouse is presumed to have been paid out of the community estate. Van Camp v. Van Camp, 53 Cal. App. 17, 199 P. 885. Family expenses are chargeable upon property of both husband and wife or either, and they may be sued jointly or separately. Haddad v. Chapin, 153 Wash. 163, 279 P. 583.

Thus, a piano purchased by the wife after the husband had refused, even though such instrument was used by the family, did not make it a "family expense" chargeable either upon husband's property or that of the community. Bush & Lane Piano 

Co. v. Woodard, 103 Wash. 612, 175 P. 329. And to the same effect, where wife purchased a piano which was never used by the family same was held not to be a family expense chargeable against the community. Jones-Rasquist-Killen Co. v. Nelson, 98 Wash. 539, 167 P. 1130.

Section 52-Bills and Notes. 

The fact that a note is given by the husband alone will not negative the presumption that it was given in relation to community property, especially where the complainant in an action thereon alleges that it was given for a community debt. Bierer v. Blurock, 9 Wash. 63, 36 P. 975.

Any liability incurred by the husband in the prosecution of any business is prima facie a charge against the community; and the presumption to that effect will continue in force until it is overthrown by proof that such liability was not incurred in any business of which the community would have had the benefit, if profit had been realized therefrom. McDonough v. 

Craig, 10 Wash. 239, 38 P. 1034. 

Where a promissory note is executed by the husband as principle, it raises a presumption in favor of the community character of the debt. Reed v. Loney, 22 Wash. 433, 61 P. 41.

Note executed by husband, a trustee of a company to provide funds for the manager's expenses in trying to obtain a contract, under which timber from community lands would have been purchased, held a community debt for which the community was liable. Peter v. Hensen, 86 Wash. 413, 150 P. 611.

Notes signed by the husband alone for money borrowed

for the benefit of the community, are obligations of the community. McLean v. Burginger, 100 Wash. 570, 171 P. 518.

A note given by a married man for the benefit of a corporation in which he is a stockholder is a community obligation if the corporate stock is the property of the community. Henning v. Anderson, 121 Wash. 53, 207 P. 1048.

Community is not liable on a note signed by only one member thereof as accommodation, where community received no benefit. Peterson v. Zimmerman, 142 Wash. 385, 253 P. 642. 

Where a married woman executes her negotiable promissory note and receives the money therefor, and expends the same for her son and for medical attendance upon herself, and subsequently renews the note after it is transferred in due course to an innocent holder, the obligation will be regarded as having been given for her own use and benefit, or for the use and benefit of her separate estate. Overland Nat. Bank v. Halveston, 33 Idaho, 489, 196 P. 217. 

Where a married woman executes her negotiable note in payment of an assessment upon bank stock purchased by her and carried in her name, she will be estopped from setting up the defense that such note was given for a community indebtedness and is therefore void as against her. Id. 

A wife, to secure her husband's debt, joined with him in a note, and in a mortgage upon her separate property, induced by his representations that she became liable only to the extent of such property. The mortgagee took a collateral agreement from others, to which the wife and her husband were not parties, to secure any deficiency beyond the value of the property mortgaged. Held, that the wife was bound as principal, and that her liability was not restricted by the understanding with her husband or the collateral security taken. Alexander v. Bouton, 55 Cal. 15.

Section 53-Guaranty and Suretyship. 

A guaranty by a husband, who is a stockholder in a corporation, of the payment of goods to be furnished the corporation, being merely a contract of suretyship, creates a separate, and not a community debt. Spinning v. Allen, 10 Wash. 570, 39 P. 151.

In the case of Horton v. Donohoe Kelly Banking Co., 46 P. 

409, 15 Wash. 399, the Supreme Court of Washington said: "Here the surety had a substantial interest in the corporation, which he held for the benefit of the community. Hence, when he saw fit to incur liability as a surety, for its benefit, it will not be presumed that it was from pure friendship to the corporation, but rather for the purpose of protecting his interest therein. Hence the liability incurred was in the course of business, and this business did not relate to his own separate estate, but to property rights belonging to the community. 

If, to aid the corporation in which he was thus interested, he had performed services, and such services had resulted in a benefit to the corporation, such benefit would have inured to the community, and not to the husband alone. This being so, the converse must be true, and liability incurred for the benefit of the corporation should be enforceable against the property of the community."

The community real property is liable upon a suretyship obligation entered into by the husband alone for the benefit of the community personalty, by the giving of an appeal bond upon an appeal from a judgment against a corporation in which he had invested community property as a stockholder, although such investment was made against the proceeds of the wife. 

Floding v. Denholm, 40 Wash. 463, 82 P. 738.

A husband's liability as surety on a bond in receivership, brought for the benefit of community property, held a liability of the community. Williams v. Hitchcock, 150 P. 1143, 86 Wash. 536.

The husband's gratuitous bond is not a community debt where it was not given in a community enterprise. Kanters v. Kotick, 102 Wash. 523, 173 P. 329. 

In the case of Lincoln Trust Company v. Spangler, 121 

Wash. 267, 209 P. 521, the Washington Court said: "It is true that the community cannot be held for obligation which is purely one of suretyship and not of benefit to the community, but if the community receives the benefit, even though not the entire benefit, the community becomes obligated." 

Where a married man's stock subscription was partly paid for by his separate property and the balance was not ultimately

www.* لات paid from any separate property, it will be presumed that the balance of the stock was community property at the time it was acquired; and hence his guaranty of a corporate debt would be a community obligation. Spokane State Bank v. Wells, 139 

Wash. 47, 245 P. 401.

Section 54-Torts. 

The Washington Court, in the case of Floding v. Denholm, 40 Wash. 463, 82 P. 738, said: "So that the rule now is that community property is liable for a debt created by the husband for the benefit of the community. But such property is not liable for a debt created by tort of either spouse, or one which is not for the benefit of the community." 

Where a married man, engaged in the real estate business acted as plaintiff's agent in the purchase of property, and misrepresented the price paid, thereby wrongfully making a profit over and above his commissions, the principal may recover a community judgment against the agent and his wife for the difference between the price actually paid by the agent and the amount represented by him as paid; since the wrongful profit made by the agent would be community property. McGregor v. Johnson, 58 Wash. 78, 107 P. 1049, 27 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1022.

Where a customer was injured by the negligence of a husband in operating a store belonging to the community of himself and wife, the damages recovered against the husband constituted a community debt. Woste v. Rugge, 122 P. 988, 68 Wash. 90.

Community property is not subject to the lien of a judgment recovered against the husband for a tort which was not committed for the benefit of the community. Wilson v. Stone, 90 

Wash. 365, 156 P. 12.

Neither the husband nor wife having any independent proprietary interest in the community property, either real or personal, Rem. Code, Sec. 5917, giving the husband the management and control of the community personal property, with like power of disposition as he has of his separate personal property, must be construed to be a management and control in the community interest, and merely vests him with absolute discretion in the voluntary disposition of the community personalty, and does not subject the same to involuntary sale to satisfy the husband's separate debts (overruling Powell v. Pugh, 13 Wash.

577, Gund v. Parke, 15 Wash. 393, and Morse v. Estabrook, 19 Wash. 92). Schramm v. Steele, 97 Wash. 309, 166 P. 634.

A client's action against an attorney and his wife to recover of the community a sum collected by the attorney and wrongfully withheld, is not for a tort for which the attorney alone would be liable; the presumption being that the ordinary business of the husband was community business and the complaint need not negative the exceptions to the rule. Henrickson v. Smith, 111 Wash. 82, 189 P. 550.

Where money of a corporation is applied by an agent to the wrongful payment of his private debt to a married woman, her liability for the repayment of the money to the corporation arises on an implied contract for money had and received, and not for a tort, and the community of husband and wife is liable therefor. Farmers Market v. Austin, 118 Wash. 103, 203 P. 42.

Community is liable for tort of husband in fraudulently securing money for corporation in which he was interested. 

Blouen v. Quimpere Canning Co., 247 P. 940, 139 Wash. 436.

Husband in committing tortious acts in conduct of community business is presumed to act as agent of community, unless exceeding authority. De Phillips v. Neslin, 283 P. 691, 155 

Wash. 147.

Tort by husband in managing community business renders community property liable. Bortle v. Osborne, 285 P. 425, 155 

Wash. 585.

Section 55.-Rights of Creditors. 

The Arizona court in the case of Rundle v. Winters, 298 P. 

929, said: "When spouses have treated the income from their separate property as community, and it was their intent that it should become community, the character of the income changes in accordance with their intention. Volz. v. Zang, 113 Wash. 378, 194 P. 409; In re Brown's Estate, 124 Wash. 

273, 214 P. 10; Jacobs v. Hoitt, supra. Nor can subsequent creditors attack such an agreement. Yake v. Pugh, 13 Wash. 78, 42 P. 528, 52 Am. St. Rep. 17."

A judgment rendered in this state upon a debt created in the State of Wisconsin, which, by the laws of that state, was the separate individual debt of the husband, and enforcible

only against his separate property, cannot be satisfied out of community real property acquired by husband and wife in this state long after the debt arose. La Selle v. Woolery, 14 Wash. 

70, 44 P. 115, 53 Am. St. Rep. 855.

In proceedings supplemental to execution, rent of the wife's separate property held not a charge on the community property prior to the claim of the husband's judgment creditor. Peterson v. Badger State Land Co., 86 Wash. 530, 150 P. 1187.

In an action to set aside a deed from husband to wife, as fraudulent as to community creditors existing at the time of the conveyance, the evidence is insufficient to show that the property was the separate property of the wife, where the transaction can only be sustained as a ratification of a prior parol gift of the husband's community interest in real estate, taken in the wife's name after coverture and presumptively community property, and upon the faith of which credit was given by existing creditors in the belief that it was community property. Union Savings & Trust Company of Seattle v. Manney, 101 Wash. 274, 172 P. 251.

In such action, where the spouses had nothing at the time of marriage, it was incumbent upon the wife to show that the property was acquired by gift; and an oral gift of real estate, void under Rem. Code, Sec. 8745, requiring the same to be by deed, cannot be shown by the fact that real estate, acquired after coverture, was taken in the wife's name, when less than one-tenth of the purchase price was traced to the wife's separate personal property; since the same was presumptively community property, and its status when acquired remains the same until divested by deed or estoppel; and since if the attempted gift by parol was void, any subsequent attempted ratification by deed would also be void as to creditors then existing. Id.

The presumption that property, once separate, remains so, will not be overthrown by an alleged understanding between husband and wife, in the absence of any agreement between them to that effect, especially as against existing creditors. 

Jacobs v. Hoitt, 119 Wash. 283, 205 P. 414.

An executory contract for the purchase of real estate by a community, being personal property, the husband's quit claim

is good faith of the community interest to his wife, before any judgment lien is secured by a community creditor, makes it her separate property which such creditor cannot follow. Norman v. Levenhagen, 142 Wash. 372, 253 P. 113.

The exemption of community property from liability for a separate and individual debt of the husband extends to a judgment against him rendered in another state for an individual debt long before the community property was acquired. La- 

Selle v. Woolery, 14 Wash. 70, 44 P. 115.

Business debts incurred by a husband are chargeable upon the community property unless the business is shown not to have been conducted by him for the benefit of the community. 

Oregon Improvement Co. v. Sagmeister, 4 Wash. 710, 30 P. 1058.

No agreement between the husband and wife as to their community property will be good as against creditors. Green v. Ferguson, 62 Tex. 525.

The husband may become a creditor of the community for the amount of his separate fund invested for the benefit of the community. Gee v. Thompson, 41 La. Ann. 348.

Where the husband seeks to recover as a creditor of the community there must be substantial proof that such money was actually invested in the community by him. Foreman's 

Succession, 38 La. Ann. 700; Boyer's Succession, 36 La. Ann. 506.

Section 56-Exemptions. 

Section 7 of the Act in the last sentence thereof reads: "The husband and wife, and each of them, shall be entitled to the exemptions to which they, or either of them are now entitled under the laws existing prior to the enactment of this Act." 

This section keeps intact all of the rights of exemptions which a husband and wife had prior to the enactment of the Community Property Law and requires no discussion. 

However, the Community Property Act has created a new and distinct type of exemption which did not exist prior to its enactment. The Act specifically states that neither the husband nor the wife shall be liable for the debts or torts of the other and exempts community property standing in the name of either the husband or wife from the debts or liabilities of the

other. If permitted to stand this exemption will enable either the husband or the wife to place community property in the name of the other and thus avoid the payment of certain debts. 

Section 8 of the Act provides "that a creditor may satisfy his claim out of the community property which was under the management, control and disposition of the spouse incurring the indebtedness, and which has been subsequently conveyed or transferred to the other spouse, and is under the management, control and disposition of said other spouse, without proof that said creditor relied upon said community property in advancing said credit." This Section apparently does not cover property acquired any other way except by transfer from the spouse creating the debt and is not sufficient to protect the creditor where property has been purchased or acquired and the conveyances taken directly in the name of the other spouse without any intervening transfer from the spouse creating the debt. 

It is the opinion of the authors that the Supreme Court will not give this interpretation to the Act but will allow the creditor the right to follow such property through all of its changes where such property is acquired, held or conveyed in any manner so as to operate as a fraud on the creditors. 

Section 57.-Effect of Change of Control. 

The reader should keep in mind always that the community and all of its property may have been placed by the district court under the control and management of either of the spouses as discussed in chapter 7 of this book. It follows as a corrollary from such change of control that all of the debts and liabilities of either the husband or wife would become the debts and liabilities of the spouse under whose control the total of the community had been placed. 

Section 58-Divorce Proceedings. 

It should also be remembered that the rights of creditors will be changed or materially affected by divorce proceedings, and the subsequent division of property arising out of such divorce, and that the death of one of the spouses makes an entirely different situation for the creditor of the deceased spouse or the creditor who is looking to the community for the payment of his debts.

Section 59-Actions and Parties. 

No discussion has been made in this book on the question of parties or the type of action which should be brought in our courts to enforce the collection of community debts as it is not believed that our present community property law makes any material changes in this respect. We assume that both the husband and wife should be joined in any action which seeks to enforce a community debt or to recover jointly owned property or community property standing in the name of one spouse only. Where any action is brought to subject any portion of either the separate property of the spouses, the community property, or any property alleged to be a part of the community, or alleged to be separate property where apparently appearing to be a part of the community property, and such action is based upon some circumstances indicating fraud, the proper procedure would, of course, be to bring such action against both the husband and wife and any person claiming any title or interest in and to such property in order that the court might settle the equities or subject such property to process in satisfaction of indebtedness. The same reasoning will apply where a husband and wife have made mutual conveyances of such community property to one another or have entered into any type of contract giving to either of them a larger measure of control than follows automatically from the operation of the community property law. All of such conveyances and contracts must necessarily be subject to the rights of creditors, third parties, and innocent purchasers.

The right to attach community property or to subject any of its proceeds to garnishment as between the husband and wife are not changed by the Community Property Law. 

Section 60-Bankruptcy. 

The bankruptcy courts have had occasion to deal with community property in a few cases and for the benefit of the reader we cite the following cases. Lynch v. Speed, 7 Am. B. 

R. (N. S.) 475; Matter of Lynch, 5 Am. B. R. (N. S.) 650; 

Greene v. Levinson, 1 Am. B. R. (N. S.) 483; Hanna v. Swift, 22 Am. B. R. (N. S.) 398. 

---

Chapter Seven CONTROL AND MANAGEMENT Section 61-Control and Management of Husband and Wife. 

One of the main differences between the Oklahoma Community Property Law and that of the other states lies in the matter of control and management. Without entering into a detailed discussion of the methods applicable in other states, we shall devote this chapter to a discussion of the statutory provisions fixing the control and management of community property in Oklahoma.

Sections 3 and 4 of the Act provide as follows: 

"The husband shall have the sole management, control and disposition of his separate property, both real and personal, to the extent permitted by law as to his property prior to the enactment of this Act."

The only difference in the two sections is that one refers to the husband and the other to the wife. 

These two sections must be construed in connection with the following provision of Section 5: 

"The wife shall have the management and control and may dispose of that portion of the community property consisting of her earnings, all rents, interest, dividends, income and other profits for her separate estate and all other community property the title to which stands in her name. The husband shall have the management and control and may dispose of all other community property. ***"

It is clear from the wording of the Act that the wife has complete control over, and power of disposition of, her separate property and that portion of the community which is made up of income for her estate, as well as all community property standing in her name. The phrase "for her separate estate" in the last quoted section appears to be a clerical error either for the word "of" or the word "from"; otherwise it is

without clear meaning. The husband's control is similar to that of the wife except it is wider by extending over community property standing in the name of both the husband and wife, as well as that in his name.

It is not believed these sections make any change whatever in the statutes prescribing the powers and authority of the husband and wife existing prior to the enactment of the community property law, except as to community property which is not under the control of the wife. Control of the husband over property standing in the names of the husband and wife gives him the power of disposing of same. This power he did not possess previous to the Community Property Act. 

Section 62-Relation of Husband and Wife to Community. 

It is necessary to determine the exact relation which the husband or the wife, or both together, bear toward one another and the community property in order to ascertain the exact nature of their powers of management, control and disposition. 

Inasmuch as it is believed the Oklahoma court will tend to follow both the theories used in the State of Washington and the State of Texas, rather than those of other states, we shall confine our discussion to the decisions of these two states. 

In the State of Washington the theory that the husband and wife constitute an entity separate and apart from each other has been repeatedly adopted by their courts. Ostheller v. 

Spokane I. E. Ry. Co., 107 Wash. 678, 182 P. 630; Olive v. 

Meek, 103 Wash. 467, 175 P. 33; Wasmund v. Wasmund, 90 

Wash. 274, 156 P. 3; Holyoke v. Jackson, 3 P. 841. In this last named case the Supreme Court of the State of Washington said: "This creature is sometimes, though inaccurately, denominated species of partnership. It probably approaches more nearly to that kind of partnership called universal than to any other business relationship known to the civil or common law. "A conventional community, in a state where statutes would permit, might be contrived which would be substantially a partnership; but an ordinary legal community is, in many important particulars, quite distinct. It is like a partnership in that some property coming from or through one or another or both of the individual forms for both a common stock which bears the 37

MAAAA^^^^^^AMI losses and receives the profits of its management, and which is liable for individual debts; but it is unlike in there is no regard paid to proportionate contribution, service or business fidelity; that each individual, once in it, is incapable of disposition of his or her interest; and that both are powerless to escape from the relationship, to vary its terms, or to distribute its assets or its profits. In fixity of constitution a community resembles a corporation. It is similar to a corporation in this, also that the state originates it, and that its powers and liabilities are ordained by statute. In it the proprietary interest of husband wife are equal, and those interests do not seem to be united merely but unified; not mixed or blent, but identified. 

It is sui generis—a creature of the statute. By virtue of the statute this husband and wife creature acquires property. That property must be procurable, manageable, convertible, and transferable in some way. In somebody must be vested a power in behalf of the community to deal with and dispose of it. To somebody it must go in case of death or divorce. 

Its exemptions and liabilities as to indebtedness must be defined. All this is regulated by statute. Management and disposition may be vested in either one or both of the members. 

If in one, then that one, not thereby made the holder of larger proprietary rights than the other, but is clothed, in addition to his or her proprietary rights, with a bare power in trust for the community. "Community property was created by statute of December 2, 1869, although called 'common property' by that statute, and by the statute of 1873. By the statute of 1879 it was first denominated 'community property.' The right referred to in the twenty-first section of the Act of 1879 are proprietary rights and not such naked power of management control or disposition as are devolved upon any married person to be exercised in trust for the community. Therefore, all the provisions of the Act of 1879 which operate to create, vary, or assign powers of that description 'are applicable to property previously acquired.' By Section 8 of the statute of 1879, the husband is clothed with a certain trust in respect to community real property. The management and control of it is vested in him, not for himself, but for the community. Besides this, he with the wife, is endowed with power to dispose of it. This power, too, is in trust for the community; for we must distinguish the community called into existence by the statute from the

two individuals who compose it. By a like distinction a corporation is conceived to differ from its stockholders. When a husband, therefore, thus situated, undertakes to dispose of the community property of his own, he stands differently from the absolute proprietor of a homestead, who has power to shift the domicile of a family at pleasure, and who is required to have his wife join, not because she is joint trustee with him, nor because he, without her, is not owner, but merely as a check on his power of disposition, lest certain consequences follow injurious to the proper policy of the state. In the matter of disposing of community real property, husband and wife are, by law of 1879, joint trustees for their mutual benefit in the community. Within the scope of their joint trust neither can act without the other. No contract of disposition undertaken by either husband or wife, in contravention of his or her fiduciary relationship to community real property, can be enforced so as to reach any such property directly or indirectly. And if, at the time of making the supposed contract, the would be purchaser knows it to be in breach of fiduciary duty, then it is void in every aspect, and cannot be the foundation of any liability whatever."

The husband is the agent of the entity and not of the individuals. Marston v. Rue, et al., 92 Wash. 129, 159 P. 111.

Under the entity theory and the proprietary equality of husband and wife the community property is not liable for the separate obligations of the husband whether arising from contract, Case Threshing Machine Co. v. Wiley, 89 Wash. 301, 154 P. 437, or growing out of a tort. Schramm v. Steele, 97 

Wash. 309, 166 P. 634.

These holdings are in line with the provisions of our statutes which expressly exempt the community from liability for the debts or torts of either the husband or wife. 

In 23 Tex. Jur., Par. 80, Page 101, we find the following statement of the Texas theory: "The connubial partnership is a real partnership so far as title and beneficial rights are concerned. The control and limited liability imposed by the statutes are exceptions grafted on to the ordinary laws governing partnerships. The beneficial title of each to the community is identical. The rights of husband and wife are equal." Citing the following cases: Moody v. Smoot, 78 Tex. 118, 14 S. W.

285; Merrell v. Moore, 47 Civ. App. 200, 104 S. W. 514; Davis v. Davis, 186 S. W. 775; Briggs v. McBride, 190 S. W. 1123; 

Burnham v. Hardy Oil Co., 108 Tex. 555, 195 S. W. 1139; Lee v. Lee, 112 Tex. 392, 247 S. W. 828; Coleman v. Coleman, 293

S. W. 695. 

It has long been settled in Texas that the interests of the husband and wife in the community are equal, the husband having the right of control and disposition during the marriage except in case of the homestead. Upon the death of the wife her interest passes by operation of law to her heirs, subject to administration, and to the right of the husband to "wind up the community affairs." Veramendi v. Hutchins, 48 Tex. 531; 

Johnson v. Harrison, 48 Tex. 257; Bell v. Schwarz, 56 Tex. 353.

It is settled law in Texas that the interests of the husband and wife in community property are equal, whether the deed be taken in the name of either, or the names of both. Fullerton v. Doyle, 18 Tex. 3; Higgins v. Johnson, 20 Tex. 389; 

Mitchell v. Marr, 26 Tex. 329; Cooke v. Bremond, 27 Tex. 457; 

Veramendi v. Hutchins, 48 Tex. 531; Zimpelman v. Robb, 53 

Tex. 274; Cullers v. James, 66 Tex. 494, 1 S. W. 314; Edwards v. Brown, 68 Tex. 329, 4 S. W. 380, 5 S. W. 87.

It is believed to be a doctrine thoroughly incorporated into our legal system, that the interest of the husband and wife in community property is equal, and that it is immaterial whether the grant or deed thereto be in the name of the husband or wife separately, or to them jointly. Veramendi v. Hutchins, 48 Tex. 531. husband and wife share equally the community property and its profits, regardless of proportion contributed. Wheat v. 

Owens, 15 Tex. 241.

Section 63-Vested Interests of Husband and Wife. 

Under Section 6 of the Oklahoma Community Property Law the husband and wife each have a vested undivided one-half interest in all the community property. The same section provides that the husband and wife shall each have the management, control and disposition of that portion of the community property consisting of their earnings, rents, dividends, income, and other profits, which stands in their separate names, giving the husband a like power over such community property as does

not stand in his wife's name. These two provisions seem to be in conflict as the creating of a one-half vested interest in property would seem to prevent either half owner from disposing of same without the consent and joint conveyance of the other party. For example: If the wife has community property standing in her name, one-half of which is vested in her husband, the Act provides that she may sell such property with full power without being joined therein by her husband and vice versa. It is difficult to see how our Supreme Court will reconcile these conflicting provisions so as to harmonize them.

It is plain from the wording of the Oklahoma statutes and the Community Property Law that the wife has the complete power of control, management and sale of her separate estate and that portion of the community which stands in her name. This control, of course, is absolute: She may collect rents from her separately owned property, dividends from stock, her salary or wages, as well as the timber, hay, minerals and oil and gas, and she may manage and dispose of these various fruits without accounting to her husband or the community. 

In addition to this she has control over and the power to dispose of that portion of the community property which stands in her name. This property may be either real or personal and consist of lands and buildings or stocks and bonds and other types of intangible property. It follows that she can encumber such property with mortgages and make same liable to liens without the consent of her husband. However, it is doubtful if she has this power over that portion of the community which stands in her name. If certain equities or rights of the husband are involved in such property it may be the wife will be unable to sell, encumber or burden with liens without the consent of the husband.

The same power of control exercised by the wife over her separately owned property and a portion of the community is exercised by the husband as to his separately owned property with this exception, the husband has control, not only over community property which stands in his name, but over jointly owned community property. If this provision of the law should be held valid it will grant the husband power to sell jointly owned property not heretofore within his power. 

It is impossible to cite cases in point from other states in

AMA connection with this phase of our law for the reason that Oklahoma stands alone in its provisions as to the power of management and control under the Community Property Law. If our court should hold that the community constitutes an entity separate, apart and distinct from the husband and wife, it will naturally modify the above sections fixing the control and management. Such questions as the following will arise and require solution: Is the husband the agent of the community when dealing with community property? Is the wife the agent of the community when dealing with such community property as stands in her name? Is the community a type of partnership in this State? Do any phases of trust relation arise between husband and wife in connection with community property? 

In a previous chapter it was stated that the Oklahoma Community Property Law has elements of partnership in it and that the partnership theory may prevail in this State. If it does, then new and different types of problems will arise and some of the laws of this State governing partnerships will be applied. 

Section 64 Change of Control. 

In connection with the right of control and management of the husband and wife we cite the following sections of the 

Law: 

"Section 11. Insanity or Conviction of Felony-Rights of 

Spouse. Whenever the husband or the wife is non compos mentis, or has been convicted of a felony or sentenced to imprisonment for a period of more than one year, or whenever the husband has abandoned his wife and family and left her and his family, if they have children, without support, or whenever the husband or the wife is an habitual drunkard, or for any other reason is incapacitated to manage, control, or dispose of the community property, the other spouse may present a petition, duly verified, to the district court of the county wherein they reside, or if they are nonresidents wherein any of the community property is located or situated, stating the name of the incapacitated spouse, a description of all community property, both real and personal, and the fact which renders the other spouse incapacitated to manage, control or dispose of the community property, and praying that the spouse filing the petition be substituted for the incapacitated spouse as to the management, control or disposition of the community property then under the management control and disposition of

said spouse with the same power of managing, controlling and disposing of the community property as was vested in the incapacitated spouse. "Section 12. Service of Process. In all such cases service of process shall be had as in other civil actions, provided, however, that where it is alleged that the other spouse is non compos mentis, a guardian ad litem shall be appointed having such powers as in other civil actions. 

"Section 13. Hearing on Petition. Upon the hearing of the petition so filed, the court shall render judgment therein either dismissing said petition or adjudging the spouse filing same to have such power of managing, controlling and disposing of the community property, either real or personal, formerly under the management, control and disposition of the other spouse as to the court may appear to be just, proper, equitable, and to the best interests of said estate. "Section 14. Judgments. All judgments rendered as in the preceding Section provided shall be recorded in the office of the county clerk of the county where any property affected thereby is situated and such judgment when so rendered shall be notice of the facts therein set out." 

Section 65-Sale and Management of Property. 

The above sections of the Oklahoma Community Property Law should be construed in connection with the following sections of the law relating to husband and wife: "Management, sale or incumbrance of property of one by the other, when. In case the husband or wife abandons the other and removes from the State, and is absent therefrom for one year, without providing for the maintenance and support of his or her family, or is sentenced to imprisonment either in the county jail or State penitentiary for the period of one year or more, the district court of the county or judicial subdivision where the husband or wife so abandoned or not imprisoned resides, may, on application by affidavit of such husband or wife, setting forth fully the facts, supported by such other testimony as the court may deem necessary, authorize him or her to manage, control, sell or incumber the property of the said husband or wife for the support and maintenance of the family, and for the purpose of paying debts contracted prior

to such abandonment or imprisonment. Notice of such proceedings shall be given the opposite party, and shall be served as summons are served in ordinary actions." (Section 1662, O. S. 1931) "Contracts binding on both-Liability for acts-Suits and proceedings. All contracts, sales or incumbrances made by either husband or wife by virtue of the power contemplated and granted by order of the court as provided in the preceding section, shall be binding on both, and during such absence or imprisonment the person acting under such power may sue and be sued thereon, and for all acts done the property of both shall be liable, and execution may be levied or attachment issued thereon according to statute. No suit or proceedings shall abate or be in any wise affected by the return or release of the person confined, but he or she may be permitted to prosecute or defend jointly with the other." (Section 1663, O. S. 1931) "Order set aside, when. The husband or wife affected by the proceedings contemplated in the two preceding sections, may have the order or decree of the court set aside or annulled by affidavit of such party, setting forth fully the facts and supported by such other testimony as the court shall deem proper. Notice of such proceedings to set aside and annul such order must be given the person in whose favor the same was granted, and shall be served as summons are served in ordinary actions. The setting aside of such decree or order shall in no wise affect any act done thereunder." (Section 1665, O. S. 1931)

It is believed that the provision of our statute quoted above, Section 1664, O. S. 1931, will apply to the proceedings had under the Community Property Law where the husband or wife, as the case may be, is no longer incapacitated under the statutes. The judgment of the court setting aside the order authorizing either the husband or wife to manage and sell the homestead, community property, or separately owned property, should, of course, be filed in any county where any such property exists in order to clear the record title. 

Section 66-Homestead-Sale-Insane Spouse. 

Inasmuch as Section 6 of the Act provides that "The homestead, if community property, shall not be sold, encumbered, or otherwise disposed of, except in the manner as is provided by

law prior to the enactment of this Act *** we print herein the applicable sections of the Oklahoma Statutes governing the sale and disposal of the homestead. , "Necessity of writing and signing-Homestead-Husband and wife must join. No deed, mortgage or other conveyance relating to real estate or any interest therein, other than for a lease for a period not to exceed one year, shall be valid unless reduced to writing and subscribed by the grantors; and no deed, mortgage or contract relating to the homestead exempt by law, except a lease for a period not exceeding one year, shall be valid unless in writing and subscribed by both husband and wife, where both are living and not divorced, or legally separated except to the extent hereinafter provided." (Section 9661, O. S. 1931) "When husband or wife may convey homestead. Where the title to the homestead is in the husband, and the wife voluntarily abandons him for a period of one year or from any cause takes up her residence out of the State, he may convey, mortgage or make any contract relating thereto without being joined therein by her; and where the title to the homestead is in the wife and the husband voluntarily abandons her, or from any cause takes up his residence out of the State for a period of one year she may convey, mortgage or make any contract relating thereto without being joined therein by him." 

(Section 9663, O. S. 1931) 

"Husband or wife of insane spouse may sell or convey homestead. In case of a homestead, if either the husband or wife shall become hopelessly insane, upon application of the husband or wife not insane to the district court of the county in which the homestead is situated, and upon due proof of such insanity, the court may make an order permitting the husband or wife not insane to sell and convey or mortgage such homestead." (Section 9664, O. S. 1931) "Verified petition to be filed. The applicant shall present and file in the court a verified petition setting forth the name and age of the insane husband or wife; a description of the premises; the county in which it is situated; and such facts in addition to that of the insanity of the husband or wife relating to the circumstances and necessities of the applicant and his

family as he may rely upon in support of the petition." (Section 

9665, O. S. 1931) "Copy of petition to be served. At least thirty days before the hearing of the petition, the applicant or his attorney shall serve a copy of such petition upon the nearest male relative of such insane husband or wife, resident in this State, and in case there be no such male relative known to the applicant, a copy of such petition shall be served upon the county attorney of the county in which such homestead is situated; and it is hereby made the duty of such county attorney upon being served with a copy of such petition, to appear in court and see that such application is made in good faith and that the proceedings thereon are fairly conducted." (Section 9666, O. S. 1931) "Order of sale to be entered of record. If the court shall make the order authorizing the sale of the homestead as herein. provided, the same shall be entered upon the minutes of the court and thereafter the sale, conveyance or mortgage made in pursuance of such order shall be as valid and effectual as if the property affected thereby was the absolute property in fee simple of the person making such sale, conveyance or mortgage." (Section 9667, O. S. 1931)

The above quoted sections of the Community Property Law provide for the wife or husband to manage, control and sell community property under certain circumstances while the sections of the statutes above quoted provide a method whereby he or she may manage, control and dispose of the separately or jointly owned property of both, hence, these statutes are complementary and not in conflict.

Chapter Eight DIVORCE Section 67-Divorce-Dissolution of Community. 

Section 1 of the Oklahoma Community Property Act reads as follows: 

Husband and Wife-Election. This Act, shall be available only to and apply only to husbands and wives and to their property for a period of time from the first day of the month in any year subsequent to the filing their written election to come under the terms of this Act until either an absolute decree of divorce is rendered dissolving their marriage, or until the death of one of them. (Sec. 1, Art. 2, Chap. 62, S. L. 1939) Section 10 of the Act provides: 

Divorce Division of Property. In the event of the dissolu tion of marriage by decree of any court of competent jurisdiction, community property shall be divided between the parties by the court granting the decree, in such proportions as such court, from the facts in the case, shall deem just and equitable, and such division shall be subject to revision on appeal in all respects including the exercise of discretion by the court below. (Sec. 10, Art. 2, Chap. 62, S. L. 1939) 

The first Section of the Act quoted above provides one of the methods terminating and dissolving the community, towit, by divorce, and Section 10 provides how the community property shall be divided by the court in the event of a decree of divorce dissolving the marriage. At the outset, attention is called to the seeming conflict between the provisions of Section 10 and the first sentence of Section 6 of the Act which reads as follows: 

Property Acquired After Effective Date of Act. All property acquired by the husband or the wife after the effective date of the election to come under the terms of the Act as provided in Section 1 of this Act, except that which is separate property

۳۱۴ of either one or the other, shall be deemed the community or common property of the husband and the wife and each, subject to the provisions of this Act, shall be vested with an undivided one-half interest therein. *** (Sec. 6, Art. 2, Chap. 62, S. L. 

1939)

The above quoted section gives both the husband and wife an undivided one-half vested interest in all of the community property. Under Section 10 the court in granting a decree of divorce is authorized to divide community property between the husband and wife in such proportions as such court shall deem just and equitable. This authority given the court is in direct contradiction to the title vested in the husband and wife by section 6. If the wife owns a vested undivided one-half interest in all of the community property, and the husband has the same title as the wife, it is difficult to see how the court could divide such property in any other proportions than half and half. Neither of the sections defining the title which the parties have nor the section providing for division on divorce make any reference to the person in whose name the title stands. Other sections of the Act granting control and management are specific as to whose name the title stands in. 

Section 68-Division of Property. 

It has hitherto been the rule in Oklahoma in divorce cases to make property division upon a just and equitable basis, taking into consideration the financial condition of the parties as well as their fault in the premises. But the Oklahoma Community 

Property Act is silent on these matters and the two provisions quoted above are apparently in conflict. The authors venture the suggestion that our supreme court will be likely to follow Section 10 as that section leaves it open to the court to make such division of community property as under all the circumstances will be just and equitable. 

In connection with the sections of the Oklahoma Community Property Act cited above attention is called to the following sections of the Oklahoma Statute which should be construed together with the applicable sections of the Community Property Act in a divorce proceeding: 

Decree Refused Where Parties in Equal Wrong-Custody of Children and Division of Property. When the parties appear to be in equal wrong the court may in its discretion refuse

to grant a divorce, and in any such case or in any other case where a divorce is refused, the court may for good cause shown make such order as may be proper for the custody, maintenance and education of the children, and for the control and equitable division and disposition of the property of the parties, or of either of them, as may be proper, equitable and just, having due regard to the time and manner of acquiring such property, whether the title thereto be in either or both of said parties. (Sec. 669, O. S. 1931)

Attachment to Control Property-Expenses of Suit. After a petition has been filed in an action for divorce and alimony, or for alimony alone, the court, or a judge thereof in vacation, may make and enforce by attachment such order to restrain the disposition of the property of the parties or of either of them, and for the use, management and control thereof, or for the control of the children and support of the wife or husband during the pendency of the action, as may be right and proper; and may also make such order relative to the expenses of the suit as will insure an efficient preparation of the case; and on granting a divorce in favor of the wife or refusing one on the application of the husband, the court may require the husband or wife to pay such reasonable expenses of the other in the prosecution or defense of the action as may be just and proper, considering the respective parties and the means and property of each. (Sec. 670, O. S. 1931)

Disposition of Property-Alimony. When a divorce shall be granted by reason of the fault or aggression of the husband, the wife shall be restored to her maiden name if she so desires, and also to all the property, lands, tenements, hereditaments owned by her before marriage or acquired by her in her own right after such marriage, and not previously disposed of, and shall be allowed such alimony out of the husband's real and personal property as the court shall think reasonable, having due regard to the value of his real and personal estate at the time of said divorce; which alimony may be allowed to her in real or personal property, or both, or by decreeing to her such sum of money, payable either in gross or in installments, as the court may deem just and equitable. As to such property, whether real or personal, as shall have been acquired by the parties jointly during their marriage, whether the title thereto be in either or both of said parties, the court shall make such division

wwwwww between the parties respectively as may appear just and reasonable, by a division of the property in kind, or by setting the same apart to one of the parties, and requiring the other thereof to pay such sum as may be just and proper to effect a fair and just division thereof. In case of a finding by the court, that such divorce should be granted on account of the fault or aggression of the wife, the court may set apart to the husband and for the support of the children, issue of the marriage, such proportion of the wife's separate estate as may be proper. (Sec. 672, O. S. 1931)

Section 69-Fault of Parties. 

Where the court in a divorce action denies the decree to either party the court may make an equitable division of the property. Moody v. Moody, 120 Ok. 128, 250 P. 916. Such equitable division of property should take into consideration the time and manner of its acquisition. Davis v. Davis, 61 Ok. 

275, 161 P. 190. But such statute authority does not authorize the court to decree to an able bodied husband any interest in the separate property of the wife or the income thereof. Moody v. Moody, 120 Okl. 128, 250 Pac. 916.

Our court has held in the case of McAdoo v. McAdoo, 137 

Ok. 12, 277 P. 943, that the court in the wife's action for separate maintenance has authority without granting a divorce to render a proper order for the division of the property. 

Where a divorce is granted to the husband because of the fault of the wife a fair and equitable division of the property acquired by the parties' joint industry during their marriage should be made, but no division should be made of the separate property acquired by the husband prior to the marriage. Colvin v. Colvin, 183 Ok. 267, 81 P. (2d) 305; Owen v. Owen, 183 

Ok. 200, 80 P. (2d) 628.

Where a wife was granted a divorce by reason of the fault of the husband she was entitled under the statute to all property owned by her before marriage or acquired by her in her own right after marriage, and not previously disposed of. Coleman v. Coleman, 180 Ok. 574, 72 P. (2d) 369.

The Federal District Court in construing judgment rendered by an Oklahoma court in a divorce action awarding all of the property of the parties, except an automobile, to defendant

husband, was required to assume that the Oklahoma court intended to and did follow provisions of this section that a wife shall be restored to all property owned by her before marriage or acquired by her in her own right after marriage. Burton v. 

Equitable Life Assur. Soc. of U. S., D. C. Ok., 21 Fed. Sup. 62. 

That portion of the statute authorizing the division of the property of the party at fault where divorce is granted to the nonoffending party, relates to the division of individually owned property, and does not apply to that portion of the statute relating to jointly acquired property. Carter v. Carter, 181 Ok. 

204, 73 P. (2d) 404.

This statute authorizing court in granting a divorce to make division of property acquired jointly during coverture does not vest the wife with any interest in jointly acquired property prior to the divorce decree, or the institution of the divorce action, since such statute relates only to divorce. Jones v. Farris, 180 Ok. 341, 69 P. (2d) 344. 

In the case of Bertrum v. Bertrum, 184 Ok. 61, 84 P. (2d) 

598, our Supreme Court held that on the granting of a divorce to either the husband or the wife the court is not required to divide property acquired by parties jointly during marriage equally between the parties, but it is given a wide latitude in determining what part of jointly accumulated property shall be given to each. In this same case the court said: "No rule requires any fractional part of joint accumulations or husband's separate property to be allowed to the wife on divorce awarded her for husband's fault, but the division of the property depends on the circumstances."

Lands alloted to husband as a member of one of the Five Civilized Tribes was not "jointly acquired property" of husband and wife within the meaning of divorce and alimony statutes. 

Harden v. Harden, 182 Ok. 364, 77 P. (2d) 721. Nor was land purchased by husband after wife had moved away from him, "jointly acquired property" within the meaning of the divorce and alimony statutes, notwithstanding wife had joined in the execution of the purchase price mortgage. Id.

The words "just and reasonable," mean, or are equivalent to an equitable division, but such division does not mean an equal division. Carter v. Carter, 181 Ok. 204, 73 P. (2d) 404.

Section 70-Dissolution of Community and Division of Property. 

As a divorce in this state operates to effect a complete dissolution of the community if the decree of divorce is silent as to the community property, the husband and wife after such divorce would be tenants in common as to all of their community property. Southwestern Mfg. Co. v. Swan, (Tex.) 43 S. W. 813. 

In an action for a divorce the parties may seek a determination of all of their property rights; but if they do not do so in such action such property rights are not deemed adjudicated and are subject to determination in another proceeding. Callnon v. Callnon, (Cal.) 46 P. (2d) 988; Brown v. Brown, 171 Cal. 1, 147 P. 1168; De Godey v. Godey, 39 Cal. 157; 

Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Welch, 202 Cal. 312, 260 

P. 545; Tarien v. Katz, 216 Cal. 554, 15 P. (2d) 493; Taylor v. Taylor, 192 Cal. 71, 218 P. 756. But where the property rights have been put in issue in a divorce proceeding, either by specific allegations describing such property, or by allegations that no community property exists the decree is res judicata of such rights. Allen v. McCrary, 220 Cal. 508, 31 P. (2d) 388; 

Brown v. Brown, 170 Cal. 8, 147 P. 1171.

A third party who seeks or claims any interest in and to the community property may be joined in such action for divorce and the decree vesting the property rights is equally binding upon said third party and the parties to the divorce. 

Kashaw v. Kashaw, 3 Cal. 312; Weyer v. Weyer, 40 Cal. App. 

765, 182 P. 776.

In a suit for divorce where there is no allegation as to the community property and no decree on that point, the former spouses hold the property as tenants in common, subject to marital partnership debts. Le Baron v. Le Baron, (Ariz.) 205 

P. 910; Taylor v. Taylor, 218 P. 756. And community property of the husband and wife not disposed of in a decree of divorce remains undisturbed so far as the respective interests of the members of the community are concerned, and becomes common property instead of community property, and either of them may thereafter enforce their rights therein by another action; and personal property remaining in the possession of one of the spouses after a divorce decree which is silent as to its disposition constitutes no exception to the rule. Harvey v. Pocock, (Wash.) 159 P. 771.

Where in a divorce petition the wife prayed to be awarded the community property, alleged to consist of household furniture only and the decree set aside for her all community property of parties, since it was silent as to other property than that named in her complaint it operated as an adjudication that at the time the action was begun there was no other community property, and hence other property owned by the parties in so far as concerned its community character was thereby determined to be separate property of the spouses in whose name it was held. Metropolitan Life Ins. Co. v. Welch, (Cal.) 260 P. 545.

In the case of Moor v. Moor, 255 S. W. 231, the supreme court of the State of Texas held that in a decree for divorce the court shall order a division of the estate, having due regard to the rights of each party, does not deprive the wife, as a matter of law, from sharing equally in the community property, where there is no decree as to the property; the presumption that property in possession of either spouse on dissolution of the marriage is community property being applicable, whether the dissolution be by death or divorce. 

Section 71-Partition. 

Divorce decree held to partition merely property particularly awarded to each party, and a decree awarding particular property to each party does not preclude either party from demanding partition of community property not therein included. 

Kirberg v. Worrell, (Tex.) 44 S. W. (2d) 940.

Where a wife in complaint for divorce, alleged that a complete adjustment and settlement of property interests had been made, which allegation was admitted in the answer, but there was no allegation in either pleading as to a particular contract entered into between the parties with reference to the community estate prior to the institution of the divorce suit and no request by either of the parties that such contract be annulled or confirmed, and no action with reference thereto by the court, the wife was not estopped, by reason of such proceedings, from bringing an action to rescind the contract on the ground that the husband had fraudulently concealed a part of the property. 

Normile v. Denison, (Wash.) 119 P. 995.

Where the property rights of the wife were not disposed of in the divorce decree it was held in the case of Jones v.

Frazier, 201 S. W. 445, that she was entitled to recover one-half from the administratrix of her husband's estate. 

Where debts have been contracted by either spouse after the decree of divorce they should not be considered in making a new partition as such debts are not charges against the community. Dailey v. Dailey, 88 S. W. (2d) 86. And a temporary injunction may be granted at the trial court at the instance of the wife restraining the husband from encumbering or disposing of the community property until the same can be apportioned. Moffitt v. Lloyd, (Tex.) 98 S. W. (2d) 860. 

One-half of the cash surrender values cannot be held community property and awarded to the wife in a divorce decree where such policies had not matured at the time of the divorce and were still under the husband's control with power to change beneficiaries. Fain v. Fain, (Tex.) 93 S. W. (2d) 1226.

Section 72-Foreign Divorce. 

Where wife institutes a suit for a divorce in a foreign state other than the state of her husband's residence and in which he had no property and secures a decree of divorce, such decree, though treated as one in rem, dissolving the bonds of matrimony, has no binding effect in personam against the defendant husband, though he was personally served with process in the state of residence, for such service did not bring him within the jurisdiction of the foreign court. Keenan v. Keenan, (Nev.) 

164 P. 351. And in this case the court said that where the wife secured a divorce in a foreign state from her husband, who was a resident of the State of Nevada, and in which state the community property of the parties was situated, that she could not thereafter maintain an independent action in the Nevada courts to secure a division of the community property pursuant to the statute; the statute having reference only to the court granting the divorce. Where a court of a sister state grants a decree of divorce to a wife residing there and makes no disposition of the property belonging to the parties and situated in Washington where the husband resides, the courts of Washington may thereafter, in a timely suit for partition on any appropriate action, divide the property in Washington between the parties as it would do in a divorce proceeding under the statute authorizing a court

in granting a divorce to dispose of the property of the parties. 

Buckley v. Buckley, (Wash.) 96 P. 1079.

A decree of divorce, entered by the State of Oregon, provided that the husband should pay the wife a sum of $5,000 in satisfaction of her interest in such of the husband's lands as were without the state, on condition that the wife should accept such sum in full satisfaction of her interest in those lands, and file a conveyance to the husband of such interest with the clerk of the court. The wife caused execution to be issued on the decree, and sold the husband's land in Oregon to satisfy the same, which lands the husband redeemed by paying the amount of the execution to the wife. No conveyance or release of interest in the Washington lands was ever executed by the wife, as directed by the decree. Held, that such decree of the court of Oregon could not operate to transfer plaintiff's interest in community lands in Washington, nor estop her from recovering them from her husband's widow by a second marriage. Gratton v. Weber, (Wash.) 47 Fed. 852.

Where a husband procured a divorce from his wife in Nevada, where he was domiciled, such court although it had jurisdiction to grant a divorce on service by publication against the wife, who was a nonresident, had no power to settle the title of lands in California and a general finding or decree purporting to bar the rights of the wife in such real estate was ineffectual. 

Taylor v. Taylor, (Cal.) 218 P. 756.

On divorce being decreed, wife's insurable interest in her husband's life ceases, and she ceases to be a beneficiary under a policy naming her as such and the cash surrender value of such policy upon the life of the husband in which wife is beneficiary is not community property, and a one-half interest therein does not pass to the wife under a decree of divorce awarding her a half interest in the community property. Whiteselle v. 

Northwestern Mut. Life Ins. Co., (Tex.) 221 S. W. 575. 

Section 73-Liability for Debts. 

On a divorce and division of the community property between a husband and wife, the wife becomes jointly liable, to the extent of the property received by her, for the payment of a note executed by her husband during the marriage, though the husband cannot thereafter bind her by a renewal of the note. Grandjean v. Runke, (Tex.) 39 S. W. 945.

SKILL

Chapter Nine DESCENT AND DISTRIBUTION Section 74-No Administration of Community Property.
